#217. Trajectory raises $45M to make AI model training accessible to nontechnical experts

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Whether developed by OpenAI or Anthropic or built on open source alternatives, current AI models share a critical limitation: They don’t learn from their mistakes without further training.

Businesses adapting these models for specialized fields, such as law and finance, typically get around this by fine-tuning general models. However, that often requires hiring internal research teams or relying on expensive consultants, commonly known as forward-deployed engineers (FDEs).

Trajectory, a startup founded about a year ago by three Stanford classmates with experience at Google DeepMind and Apple, wants to automate that process, allowing AI models to learn from feedback and continuously improve at specialized tasks.

The 18-person company just announced that it raised $45 million in a Series A round at a $300 million valuation, led by Sequoia, with participation from Nvidia, Bessemer Venture Partners, and Bain Capital Ventures.

Trajectory co-founder Ronak Malde compared foundational models to a very smart PhD student who’s always starting a new job. “But what the economy values is someone who worked on the job for 30 years,” he told TechCrunch.

The startup aims to solve this problem through an approach known as continual learning. Businesses using Trajectory can start with an open source AI model and connect the platform to their applications, allowing it to collect feedback on how the model performs. Trajectory then uses that information to retrain the model, helping it improve at specialized tasks over time.

With Trajectory, lawyers, doctors, and other specialists can improve the models near automatically themselves, Malde said. That capability could make deploying specialized AI faster and cheaper.

Companies like Harvey and Rogo are already using Trajectory’s platform to post-train open source models, helping them perform more like experienced lawyers or financial analysts by learning from previous tasks, such as reviewing a particular type of legal contract or valuing a company using a specific methodology. Trajectory’s other customers include Decagon, Mercor, and Nvidia. The startup is now making its post-training platform publicly available.

The people using Trajectory decide “what good looks like for their organization and can turn that into improvements for their model,” Malde said.

Trajectory’s approach to continual learning shares some similarities with recursive self-improvement (RSI), the idea that AI systems can improve their own capabilities. But co-founder Michael Elabd argues that keeping humans involved in the training process makes Trajectory’s approach safer, since autonomous self-improvement could lead to unintended or even dangerous outcomes.

Trajectory isn’t the only startup trying to automate post-training. Other companies going after this market include Thinking Machines Lab, founded by former OpenAI CTO Mira Murati and the company behind the fine-tuning platform Tinker, Applied Compute, which was valued at $3.25 billion last month, and Prime Intellect, a platform that helps companies build and train their own AI agents.

Malde believes the startup’s biggest advantage is its team. He was among the handful of researchers Google DeepMind acqui-hired from Windsurf. Elabd also worked at DeepMind, while Trajectory’s third co-founder, Arjun Karanam, previously worked on generative foundation models at Apple. The startup has also recruited researchers and product designers from OpenAI, Stripe, and Netflix.

Why does design matter so much to a company focused on AI training? Karanam says the startup has developed an interface that makes post-training more accessible to its nontechnical customers.

#216. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#215. Apple says it’s ‘hard to predict’ when iPhone Duo constraints will improve

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Apple Chief Operating Officer Sabih Khan acknowledged the shipping delays plaguing the new iPhone Duo during the company’s fiscal fourth-quarter earnings call on Monday, but offered little clarity on when customers can expect relief.

The delays were just one point of concern during an earnings call filled with questions about the rising costs of memory chips, driven by soaring demand from the AI industry.

“iPhone Duo is constrained as of today. The ramp is going well, and we’re working hard to get this revolutionary new product into the hands of our customers as quickly as possible. But at this time, it’s really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance,” Khan said.

Customers who didn’t wait in line at an Apple Store or immediately jump on preorders are now seeing ship times stretch into next year. Meanwhile, Verizon has been apologizing to its customers for the delays by offering gift cards.

In an October report from Financial Times, Apple described demand for the Duo as “nothing short of extraordinary,” after the device sold out on the company’s website within hours of going on sale, pushing delivery dates into next year.

The prolonged shipping delays are notable given Apple’s reputation for its highly efficient supply chain. But the company is hardly alone in facing supply pressures. Soaring demand for memory chips from the AI industry has driven up component costs for hardware makers across the board, threatening to push device prices even higher.

Apple, too, has raised prices on a number of products, Khan noted on the call, adding that those decisions were made “reluctantly.”

In another sign of the company’s efforts to meet demand for the Duo, Apple said it’s “deepening” its partnership with Perryman, a titanium producer for supplying materials for the new foldable device.

“We’ve been thrilled with the early response from reviewers and customers, and we are working hard to get iPhone Duo into customers’ hands as quickly as we can,” Apple CEO John Ternus said during his opening remarks.

#214. Tennessee national guard trained guns on dying man after guard member shot him, video shows

Footage from prosecutors shows aftermath of shooting of Tyrin Johnson, 20, shot and killed by national guard in July

Tennessee national guard troops in Memphis last summer trained their handguns on a man as he lay dying on a sidewalk after a guard member shot him in the back, video released Monday by county prosecutors shows.

A Memphis police officer can be heard in the footage from an officer’s body camera calling for an ambulance and then asking the troops: “Hey, you guys got a medical kit or anything?”

The Shelby county district attorney’s office released the videos, which also include footage from stationary cameras and a cellphone from a bystander, as the Tennessee Bureau of Investigation continues its investigation into the 5 July shooting of Tyrin Johnson.

Johnson, 20, who had been armed, died at the scene from a single gunshot wound, according to investigators. An autopsy report shared by Johnson’s family found that he was shot in the back, contradicting police and national guard accounts that said he was facing the guard members with a gun when they fired at him.

The footage did not show Johnson’s initial encounter with the guard members or them firing at him.

The guard members were part of a federal taskforce created by Donald Trump, who last year sent troops and federal agents to Democratic-run cities that he described as overrun with crime. Tennessee’s Republican governor Bill Lee had deployed the guard to support the effort. The task force was involved in four fatal shootings between May and July.

District attorney Steve Mulroy said he has not yet decided whether to file charges in connection with the death.

A spokesperson for the Tennessee national guard did not immediately respond to email and phone messages seeking comment. Ed Yarbrough, an attorney for the two guard members, said they have no comment at this time.

Authorities said the troops were responding with local police to reports of gunshots at about 4am when they began pursuing an armed man fleeing on foot.

Footage from stationary street cameras shows a person in dark clothing firing shots through an empty parking lot at three people walking on a sidewalk. Two Memphis police officers hear gunshots nearby and start walking and then running in that direction, a body camera worn by one of the officers shows. The officers say on their radio that they are pursuing a man who is running and armed with a gun.

The video, which is blurry from the officer’s movement, captures yelling and the sound of a single gunshot, followed by several additional gunshot noises.

A Tennessee national guard report obtained by the Associated Press via a public records request said two guard members responded to the Memphis police radio call, left their vehicle and approached the suspect, who “rounded on them and pointed a tan handgun at them”. Both guard members fired their service weapons – M17 handguns – and Memphis police arrived shortly after, the report stated.

Police body camera footage shows guard members at the scene shouting “Hands!” and “Turn over!” as they point their handguns at a man lying on his back on the sidewalk.

André C Wharton, an attorney for Johnson’s family, noted that the videos do not show the moments immediately before the shooting, or the shooting itself.

“This investigation must stay focused on the moment the guardsman fired the fatal shot and whether that action was reasonable,” Wharton said.

Johnson was the father of a young child and was preparing to help lead the family construction business, his grandfather, Evaniel Johnson, said.

At least six guard members were on the scene immediately after the shooting, the body camera video shows. One can be seen moving a handgun that is on the ground until a police officer tells him to leave it alone. An officer calls for an ambulance over the radio, and a guard member begins to cut off the man’s shirt just before the video ends.

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Claudio Grossi found an old sack of unidentified seeds in his family’s attic and planted them on a whim. The wheat that grew was unusually tall, with seven ears per stem, and later testing identified it as an ancient heritage grain, Triticum turgidum. #212. Hinge tests a cheaper plan as users become more fed up paying for dating apps

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Hinge is testing a cheaper subscription plan as its parent company Match Group looks for new ways to get users to pay for dating apps at a time when people are increasingly tired of subscriptions and, in some cases, dating apps altogether.

During its third-quarter 2026 earnings call on Monday, Match Group said the new Hinge tier will include features such as additional filters and custom location boundaries, giving users more control over who they see and where they meet people.

“It’s designed for women in particular, but also for price-sensitive users,” said Steve Bailey, chief financial officer of Match Group. “Remember that Hinge is one of the highest-priced products we have. The revenue per payer, for example, at Hinge is almost twice that of Tinder, around $30. It also has one of the lower payer penetrations. Its payer penetration is only slightly more than half that of Tinder.”

Match Group didn’t share how much the new subscription will cost. TechCrunch has reached out to the company for more details.

The move comes as dating apps continue to struggle to convince people to pay for services they’re growing tired of using. Between subscription fatigue, endless swiping, and frustration with the overall dating experience, users are increasingly questioning whether premium dating features are worth the money.

By comparison, Tinder brought in $480 million in direct revenue but experienced a slight 2% decline year-over-year, although it has 8.9 million paid users, a 4% decline from a year earlier.

The lower-priced subscription could help Hinge build on that momentum by converting more users into paying customers without requiring them to spend as much upfront.

The move also marks a shift in Hinge’s subscription strategy. In 2023, the company experimented with a more expensive tier aimed at users willing to pay extra for features designed to help them get more out of the app. Now, it’s testing whether a more affordable option could appeal to a broader audience.

Still, cheaper subscriptions alone are unlikely to solve the bigger problem facing dating apps: user fatigue. Match Group says it’s seeing some signs of improvement, partly thanks to efforts to make its platforms feel less like traditional dating apps.

Recent initiatives include Group Hangouts and Events, which offer users alternative ways to connect beyond the usual one-on-one matching experience. Match Group plans to ramp up those efforts in the fourth quarter.

Another initiative on the horizon is real-time adaptive recommendations, which the company plans to begin testing in the fourth quarter. Announced during its CEO Connection presentation in September, the feature will allow Tinder to respond to user actions within seconds rather than hours, with the goal of making recommendations more relevant and personalized.

#211. LA communities kick out Big Oil

After years of advocacy from residents and legal battles with Big Oil, Los Angeles voted to ban drilling and phase out oil extraction in the city. It's the first city in the United States to kick the oil industry out.

Los Angeles has become the largest city in the US to ban oil drilling, after years of lawsuits and campaigning by residents who raised the alarm over pollution and potential health risks from nearby wells .

"Front-line communities have pushed the city for a solution for this for over a decade," said local resident Wendy Miranda, who has campaigned for years with STAND-LA, a coalition of community groups working to end neighborhood oil drilling.

The ordinance, approved by the LA City Council on October 28, puts an immediate stop to oil extraction and bans the redrilling or deepening of wells. Existing operations must be phased out within city limits over the next 20 years.

Though LA City is only part of greater Los Angeles, the move affects hundreds of active wells and thousands of inactive ones, many located in working class communities of color.

"In South LA, working-class families have had corporations drilling across the street from their homes," said council president Marqueece Harris-Dawson in a statement. "No one should have to live near an oil drilling site."

Rock Zierman, CEO of the California Independent Petroleum Association (CIPA), told DW, "CIPA believes this ordinance constitutes an unlawful taking of private property."

LA follows much smaller Culver City, also in California, in moving against urban oil production, and begins the process of ending its long, complex relationship with fossil fuels.

California's oil industry expanded rapidly in the early 20th century, when the state produced roughly a quarter of the world's oil. Lots of that so-called black gold was around LA and as the city grew, homes, schools and businesses were built around existing oil infrastructure.

Today, wells can be found beneath residential areas, close to playgrounds, along the coast and in some of the city's most densely populated and largely working-class neighborhoods. Some have been permanently sealed or plugged for decades, while others remain active or are idle.

Under California law, an idle well is one that has not produced oil for two years. Unlike plugged wells, they can potentially return to production.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Residents living near the active and inactive wells report strong odors, headaches, nausea and breathing difficulties. Another concern is air quality. Oil wells can release toxic pollutants, including benzene and hydrogen sulfide, and research has linked proximity to oil extraction with higher rates of respiratory illness and reduced lung function.

But both active and idle wells can also leak methane — a potent greenhouse gas that contributes to climate change — while residents say occasional accidents or breakages send oil flowing down city streets.

"Frontline communities have been experiencing or have experienced for decades living near these toxic sites and having to pay for that with their health," said Miranda, a policy director with Esperanza Community Housing, a nonprofit providing affordable housing services.

Miranda told DW she and her mother both have asthma. The council vote "definitely represents hope," she added.

LA City Council initially approved an ordinance to phase out oil extraction back in 2022 after successful lobbying by residents like Wendy Miranda. But oil companies challenged the measure in court, with industry groups successfully arguing that the city lacked the legal authority to impose the ban.

California subsequently passed legislation giving local governments greater power to regulate and restrict oil and gas operations, putting the possibility of a phaseout back on the table.

In an emailed statement to DW, CIPA CEO Rock Zierman said energy companies were prepared to challenge the new ordinance.

Zierman added that it would make city residents more dependent on foreign oil from countries with lower environmental standards when they are "already struggling" with high gas prices.

"It sends the jobs, investment and revenue elsewhere while leaving Californians to pay the bill," said Zierman.

The relationship between local oil production and prices at the pump is not straightforward and is influenced by a number of factors, according to the California Energy Commission, the state's main energy policy agency. Those include the cost of crude oil, refining, transportation, distribution and local taxes.

Even though the US is the world's largest oil producer, it exports and imports crude oil, as well as refined petroleum products. Because oil is traded globally, US residents are exposed to the same price fluctuations as people elsewhere, no matter how much of the fossil fuel is produced domestically.

California's oil industry and the amount of oil it produces have been steadily declining since the 1980s. In the coastal lowlands known as the LA Basin, output is 43% lower than eight years ago, with the amount of oil extracted falling more rapidly than the number of open wells.

When wells stop producing, it is up to the oil operators to remove equipment and plug them. The state agency that regulates oil and gas, CalGEM, and the LA fire department oversee plugging operations, and also seal wells for which no-one takes responsibility.

But many have not yet been plugged — some 80% of idle wells in LA have been inactive for more than eight years, and about a third for over a century.

The cost of plugging a well also varies widely, with estimates ranging from hundreds of thousands to millions of dollars. California law requires operators to submit plugging plans or face annual fines that increase with the amount of time the well has been idle.

But those fines aren't always paid. According to CalGEM's own data, the agency collected just $1 million out of $36 million (€32 million) in fines in 2025.

A report by Carbon Tracker also found that if all future profits from oil companies operating in California went to decommissioning wells, the state would still face a shortfall of $7 billion. The cost would by default fall to taxpayers, according to Carbon Tracker.

Miranda told DW that the next step for community activists is to reduce the 20-year timeline and have the oil sector "pay for this cleanup."  For now, she is says both she and her mother are hopeful that future generations "can have better health and breathe cleaner air."

#210. Trump loses supreme court bid to set aside 20,000 California ballots as justices refuse to take up emergency request – as it happened

Supreme court rules against Trump over bid to force California to set aside ballots

The US supreme court on Monday quickly rejected the Trump administration’s request to force California to set aside thousands of mail ballots from people President Donald Trump’s administration claims are noncitizens, the Associated Press reports.

The high court refused to intervene in a case involving 20,000 people the Republican administration flagged as likely noncitizens on the largely Democratic state’s voter rolls. The decision came just hours after the administration asked the supreme court to intervene.

The ruling was the latest judicial rebuke in the face of a wave of unprecedented efforts from the Trump administration to intervene in the elections process of multiple states. The midterm elections are expected to bring nationwide victories for Democrats and impose more Congressional checks on Trump’s power.

In California, the Trump administration had asked the high court to set aside the challenged ballots until the voters’ citizenship status could be verified. But officials there said that the list appeared to be filled with errors and that setting the ballots aside could effectively disenfranchise legal voters.

The high court did not detail its reasoning for rejecting the Trump administration’s request, as is common in emergency decisions. The justices previously rejected Trump’s push to restrict mail ballots around the country in September, even though the justices have sided with his administration in a series of other emergency docket appeals.

Here’s an updated evening summary of US politics news. The US midterm elections, expected to deliver significant if not historic wins for Democrats as voters’ rebuke to Donald Trump, are tomorrow. We’ll have round-the-clock-coverage for you then. In the meantime:

Trump’s justice department asked the supreme court to force Califronia election officials to set aside 20,000 mail ballots that the administration claims were submitted by non-citizens. Within hours, the supreme court had made its decision: No.

Elected officials in California celebrated the supreme court decision, which was one of two major court losses for the justice department that day: in a separate, related case, a federal judge ordered the Trump administration to stop demanding that California election officials set aside ballots, writing that “the Executive Branch has likely violated the Constitution’s Elections Clause, which commits authority over election rules to the states and Congress alone.”

But California governor Gavin Newsom said the state remained vigilant about additional inteference from Trump or his allies with the voting or ballot counting, and promised to prosecute anyone who tried to interfere with the election.

As Trump faced voter approval ratings of just 32%, close to his historic low, he posted a video reiterating his promise to all adult citizens that “If the Republicans win, you will each have $5,000 in your pocket,” a payout that would cost the government $1.2tn.

Asked about how soon the war with Iran would end, Trump gave a rambling answer that included his assertion, “this isn’t war”. And his claim that “there hasn’t been shooting for months.” Meanwhile, early news reports suggested that the US had in fact just conducted defensive strikes on some Iranian targets in the strait of Hormuz on the eve of the midterm elections.

Haley Stevens, the Democratic congresswoman who said she would now back the Republican nominee for US Senate in Michigan, defended her decision in an interview today. The representative who has faced fierce backlash for her eleventh-hour defection, also rejected any notion that she was “forced” into her decision to pull her endorsement and support Mike Rogers, the former GOP congressman.

Meanwhile, El-Sayed’s campaign announced that it received $2.4m in donations on Sunday, the day that Stevens announced she would now support Rogers. El-Sayed’s campaign that the amount is a single-day fundraising record for his Senate bid. His staff also noted that more than 26,000 people contributed for the first time on Sunday. The Cook Political Report changed its evaluation of the race from a “toss-up” to “Leans Democratic.” Fox News analysts were left hoping polling was overestimating El-Sayed’s lead, as it had during the Democratic primary earlier this year.

In Austin, Democratic senate candiate James Talarico is earning enthusiastic cheers from a crowd that seems to believe he is on the verge of a historic Texas win. The Democrat’s speech offers a mix of Texas history, music and sporting references. The ghost of Beto O’Rourke is nowhere to be seen.

Like Gina Hinojosa, the Democratic candidate for governor, Talarico identified tech mogul Elon Musk, who is building datacenters in Texas as a catalyst of corruption.

He said: “Paxton is funded by one dude. Elon Musk wants our land. He wants our water. He wants our tax dollars. He wants his very own personal United States senator. He is trying to buy Texas. But tomorrow we’re going to deliver a crystal clear message to the world’s first trillionaire. Texas is not for sale.”

Talarico said he and his campaign are the underdogs. “We are literally going up against the richest man in the world. We’re going up against the most powerful man in the world. This election is a battle between the powers that be and the powers that ought to be… They may have the money but we have the majority.”

Sara Cook, a longtime CBS News White House reporter, has highlighted one of Trump’s comments in his long response tonight to a question from Sean Hannity about what he could tell Americans about how soon the war with Iran will end.

Rather than reassurance, Trump provided something of a to-do list:

Trump told Hannity that “‘we might have to go after’ Pickaxe Mountain in Iran, calling it ‘not a big deal’ and saying could be done ‘in an hour,’” Cook noted on X.

She wrote that Trump had recently made a similar comment to her: “On Saturday Trump told me ‘we still have to take care of’ 1 or 2 sites, naming Pickaxe Mountain. ‘You have to take care of that.’”

Trump has made repeated public threats about targeting this same Iranian site before. As the Associated Press reported in July, “The site, known as Kuh-e Kolang Gaz La, or “Pickaxe Mountain,” lies on the south side of Iran’s Natanz nuclear enrichment site, which was bombed by the United States during Operation Midnight Hammer in 2025’s 12-day war between Iran and Israel. But Pickaxe Mountain has remained untouched as Iran continues to dig at the site.”

Down in the Des Moines suburbs in the early evening, as rain poured down and the sun set before 6 p.m., Sarah Booz knocked doors for Democratic candidates. On the doors, there were still a lot of undecided voters. Some told her they’d be voting for Sand and the rest of the downballot Dems, while others said they needed to do more research. One man who said he normally votes red said the gubernatorial race would probably be a last-minute decision. Another man and his daughter said they were looking up the candidates online and on ChatGPT. And in the evening, at a Holiday Inn by the Des Moines airport, much of the Republican ticket rallied, culminating in a pitch from US Senate candidate Ashley Hinson.

Chuck Grassley, the Republican US senator and the oldest sitting US senator, took digs at Democratic gubernatiorial candidate Rob Sand, saying that the state government is well-run and “we don’t need Sand in that machinery”. He also called Democrats running across the country “outlandish socialists” who want to “transform America” instead of preserve it. “I hope that you’ll do everything you can to make sure that I have a colleague from Iowa that won’t neutralize my vote,” he said, advocating for Hinson. Hinson, taking the stage in front of a bus she’s been campaigning in that was signed all over by her supporters, called her opponent, Zach Turek, “Too Liberal Turek”. “We fire liberal phonies, we don’t promote them,” she said.

In Texas, Democratic senate candidate James Talarico joined Hinojosa on stage and they held hands aloft. The crowd cheered. He began his speech with the analogy of a high school American football game.

Talarico said big money donors and super PACs have spent more money on this Senate race than any other in history. “They are scared. They are terrified. They see what we see: that we’re about to win this election tomorrow night.”

The Democrat went on to lambast his opponent, the Republican Ken Paxton. “We have an affordability crisis because we have a corruption crisis, but Austin, we can change that. In this election, in this state, in this race, we are running against the embodiment of this corrupt system.

“We are running against the most corrupt politician in Texas history. Ken Paxton represents everything that’s wrong with our politics. He doesn’t serve us. He serves himself.”

Talarico, a state representative, added: “I have a legislative record; Ken Paxton has a criminal record.”

Across Iowa on Monday, candidates and their supporters made final pitches in a set of toss-up races up and down the ballot, noting just how close these contests are - and just how pivotal the state could be for the nation’s politics. In a standing-room-only town hall in Waverly, Iowa, Democratic gubernatorial hopeful Rob Sand started off by asking people in their crowd which political party they belong to, then having people clap for all of them, followed by directing the crowd to sing “America the Beautiful” together. The bipartisan tone underscores Sand’s pitch to Iowa voters, including those who would need to cross party lines to support him: “not redder or bluer, but better and truer”. Wearing shirts that called to “rotate the crops” and vote for Democrats and saying “Iowa needs Sand”, about 150 voters turned out to the rally in the Republican-leaning area. Sand told the Guardian that he has seen throughout the hundreds of town halls he’s done a “yearning” from people that their politicians work together to solve problems, even as voters are generally angry this year. “We need to focus that anger and point it at where it ought to be pointed, which is at a broken two-choice political system that is too easily abused to allow elected officials to be corrupt and to be lazy and still hold power,” he said. “A lot of politicians want people to be angry with their neighbors.”

Greetings from a “concert rally” on a warm evening at Stubbs Waller Creek Amphitheater in Austin, an open air venue with wood floor, neon-lit bars and a classic Texan barbecue. Politicians and musicians have been appearing on a stage beneath a curving white canopy. When the crowd was asked who had voted early, many hands went up.

Gina Hinojosa, the Democratic candidate for governor of Texas, walked on stage to chants of “Gina! Gina!” and told supporters that her election against incumbent Greg Abbott is “neck and neck”.

She then took aim at tech titan Elon Musk: “We have the world’s only trillionaire right here in our state. Elon Musk does billions of dollars in business with the state of Texas. It is why I fought a lawsuit here in Travis County District Court to demand that Greg Abbott release the emails between him and Elon Musk.”

Hinojosa added: “The Greg Abbott corruption tax is why data centers have descended upon Texas and we have more than any other state in the union… Greg Abbott is the most corrupt governor in all Texas history.”

In talking live to Trump tonight, Fox News host Sean Hannity gave Trump the ultimate softball: he asked the president to recap his accomplishments in the past two years, which he did at length.

What Hannity did not do at any point was ask Trump for comment on the high-profile supreme court decision today rejecting the administration’s request to have the court intervene in California’s election process on the side of Trump’s Justice Department.

Asked about how soon the war with Iran would end, Donald Trump gave a rambling answer that included his assertion, “This isn’t war.”

“We’re not fighting a war. When was the last time we fired? …This is a very soft war,” Trump said, echoing comments he had made previously. He called $30bn spent on the war with Iran “peanuts” and later added, “There hasn’t been shooting for months.”

Fox News host Sean Hannity, who was speaking to the president live, did not ask Trump about the reports emerging this evening that directly contradicted those comments.

The US has conducted defensive strikes on some Iranian targets in the Strait of Hormuz on the eve of crucial midterm elections, the Associated Press reported, citing an unnamed US official. From the Associated Press:

double quotation markIranian state television reported that explosions were heard on Qeshm Island. The official, who wasn’t authorized to comment publicly and spoke on condition of anonymity, said “Iranians using surface to air missiles against US assets” had been targeted.

The strikes appeared to be limited as opposed to a return to full hostilities but followed Trump previously saying the US wouldn’t launch strikes on Iran before Tuesday’s midterms. The strikes were reported earlier by The Wall Street Journal.

As Donald Trump begins to speak live over the phone to Sean Hannity, in what he said would be his last public comment of the day, the Fox News host has asked the president to tout his accomplishments over the past two years.

But a poll released today suggests that Americans are not pleased with the president’s current performance. Trump’s current approval ​rating of 32% is close to his recent career low of 31%, according to a Reuters/Ipsos poll completed on Monday.

On Fox News, as Sean Hannity prepares to speak with Donald Trump about the midterm elections, a Fox analyst is suggesting-slash-hoping that current polls might once again be overestimating El-Sayed’s support in the Michigan senate race, as they did in the Democratic primary, which El-Sayed won very narrowly.

In May, Newson signed new election-protection measures into law in anticipation of potentialTrump administration interference during the midterms. #209. Y Combinator’s Garry Tan uses AI agents so much, he spends up to $200K a year on tokens

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan has, in the past, jokingly admitted that he’s suffered from “cyber psychosis” and sometimes barely sleeps because he’s so excited to be working with his AI agents. On a new episode of Walter Isaacson’s podcast The Builders, set to publish on Tuesday, Tan put a price tag on his passion.

“I spend about maybe $100[,000] to $200,000 a year on tokens,” he told Isaacson. He uses agents for countless work projects and coding tasks and has freely shared his set of 23 Claude Code skills, called gstack, on GitHub — an effort to help teach others what agents can do, he said.

On the one hand, he uses his agent the way many people use AI: to help him research Isaacson’s podcast and prep for the kinds of questions he might be asked. On the other, his agent also helps him run his life.

“It’s read all of my email and all of my Dropbox. It’s read, like, all of my Apple Notes, even my therapy notes going back 20 years,” he said.

He describes using AI agents as getting help with “almost anything that you do in your day-to-day life, and for it to do it somewhat more autonomously. Think of it as a Jiminy Cricket on your shoulder at all times. Like, almost anything you want to do, it knows you, and it’s there to help. And it will actually sort of run ahead and say, ‘What about this?’ Or it’ll help you see around corners.”

As an early OpenClaw adopter who is now a power user, Tan says one thing he’s learned is not to treat AI agents like traditional software. Some people are so worried about “repeatability and safety that they actually trade off all of the beautiful parts of working with an AI agent, which basically can be so similar to a human,” he said.

For instance, Y Combinator is also using an agent that is “rebuilding the way we operate at YC,” he said. “We now have an internal AI agent, for instance, named Stray Light that kind of has a personality, and then it has a company brain. Like it actually has the total context of every founder we’ve ever backed, anyone who’s ever emailed us, like all the applications.”

Tan, who is, naturally, an avid lover of technology (as anyone running Silicon Valley’s famed startup accelerator would be), believes personal AI agents will eventually become mainstream, if one major problem is solved: Costs have got to come down.

“It’s too expensive right now,” he said, adding that he knows the amount of money he spends on AI agents is “a crazy privilege that not everyone can do.”

But freebie agents controlled by Big Tech maybe aren’t the answer, either. When discussing the future he’d like tech to build, and how Meta as a social media company has impacted us, he said, “This is a huge company that controls the algorithm, and we all wish that they didn’t at some level,” he said. “So you could imagine a personal AI that you control, that you program.”

#208. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#206. Ukraine’s new pocket-sized gadget turns Russian drones’ cameras against them

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Ukrainian drone teams have a new way to spy on the drones that spy on them. A Kyiv equipment startup called CODE380 has been gaining attention for a pocket-sized receiver, the D·Vision SDR, that picks up live camera video from many Russian drones and shows it on a screen.

Those drones send their video over the air without any scrambling, so anyone with the right receiver can watch along. A Ukrainian unit can see where a drone is heading and what its pilot is aiming at before it hits.

According to Defense News, the device is light, at about 10 ounces, which is less than half the weight of competing Ukrainian models, one of which weighs a couple of pounds.

It also listens on the radio channels Russia’s newer Molniya drones started using for video in June. Serhii “Flash” Beskrestnov, a radio specialist who advises Ukraine’s president, said in a June Facebook post cited by Defense News that “most detectors don’t see these frequencies, or see them poorly.” He warned that putting too much trust in a detector could prove fatal.

Beskrestnov has also warned that digital video could one day replace analog, which would blind receivers like this one. The New York Times reported in August that some Molniyas are AI-guided, using Nvidia chips and emitting no signals.

#205. European supermarkets targeted in second reported cyberattack

Supermarkets across Europe have been targeted in cyberattacks once again. Three weeks after a defrosting attack in France, supermarkets in Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Luxembourg and Sweden reported freezer malfunctions.

Supermarkets in Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Luxembourg and Sweden reported anomalies in their freezer systems on Monday in an apparent cyberattack similar to one in France three weeks ago in which unidentified malign actors were accused of having hijacked refrigeration systems — setting them to defrost and spoiling fresh and frozen foods.

In Monday's incident, Swedish supermarket chains ICA and Coop said "a limited number" of shops had been hit, with ICA spokeswoman Helena Johansson saying the company is "looking into what has happened and the scale of it."

"Fridges and freezers have either stopped working entirely or have been partially impaired," Coop spokesman Carlos Cancino told the French AFP news agency. He noted that six of roughly 800 Coop stores in Sweden had been impacted, adding, "I haven't received any information that this is a cyberattack."

Dutch supermarket chain Ahold Delhaize on Monday said an overnight "IT incident" had hit freezers in several of its stores in Belgium and Luxembourg.

In Ireland, public broadcasterer RTE reported that 13 supermarkets had been impacted.

Chains Carrefour, Intermarche and Spar were targeted as well, with Carrefour's Belgium spokesman Mich Vergauwen saying, "It's a cyberattack… There's no other reason."

Belgium's prosecutor's office opened a criminal investigation into the attack, the Reuters news agency quoted a spokesperson as saying.

European Commissioner ‌for Agriculture and ‌Food ​Christophe Hansen on Monday called the attacks "a deliberate attempt to disrupt Europe's food supplies," stating that ⁠while "food ​security is ​not currently ​at risk, ‌the food waste ​and ⁠impact on businesses ⁠are ​real."

"These incidents ‌show ​that cyberattacks can disrupt services, affect ​businesses and directly affect ⁠citizens’ ​daily lives" added ​a Commission ​spokesperson.

As on October 12, Monday's disruption prompted the discarding of spoiled products and again raised concerns about food security.

The ICA Kvantum branch in Sjobo in southern Sweden said temperatures in their systems were remotely dialed up to 50 degrees Celsius (122 degrees Fahrenheit).

Sweden's National Cybersecurity Center boss John Billow told the Reuters news agency on Monday that his department is investigating.

"We have in recent weeks... identified that we have quite a lot of exposed ⁠operational technology devices in Sweden that ​we have been clear about the necessity to update or patch," he added.

That "exposed operational technology" includes everything from refrigerators and heating systems to security cameras, alarm networks and an expanding ‌range ​of devices that were once largely mechanical but are now often monitored and controlled remotely, creating new opportunities for cybercriminals as they seek to exploit security vulnerabilities.

#204. New York child dies from measles in state’s first fatal case in 30 years

New York governor says case is ‘devastating reminder of what’s at stake’ when vaccine protections are eroded

A child in rural northern New York has died after contracting measles, marking the state’s first death from the disease in 30 years, state officials announced on Monday.

The child died in Lewis county, about 93 miles (150km) north of Syracuse, New York governor Kathy Hochul said.

“I am heartbroken to learn that a child in Lewis County has died after contracting measles,” Hochul said in a statement.

Hochul linked the death to the federal government’s stance on immunisation, saying: “This is New York’s first measles-related death in 30 years, a devastating reminder of what’s at stake when Washington deliberately erodes decades of progress in protecting Americans from preventable illnesses.”

Measles is a highly contagious virus that can cause serious health complications. Although rare, deaths from measles occur in approximately one to three patients per 1,000 cases.

The US stopped the domestic spread of measles in 2000 by maintaining high vaccination rates, but they have fallen in recent years to below the 95% coverage needed to prevent outbreaks of the viral disease.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has confirmed three measles-associated deaths in the US this year, but state health departments have reported higher numbers. Pennsylvania health officials have reported six deaths this year.

Hochul declared a disaster emergency in early October because of measles cases in neighbouring states and in rural areas of New York.

The executive order extended the pool of people who can administer measles vaccines to include paramedics, midwives and others, and it allowed nurses to join doctors in ordering testing.

The CDC reported 4,588 cases of measles across the country in 2026 as of late October – more than double the tally for 2025.

Pennsylvania alone reported 1,338 cases on the same day. The CDC also reported large outbreaks in South Carolina and Utah, both of which are reporting more than 500 cases.

Robert F Kennedy Jr, the secretary of the US department of health and human services (HHS), has faced criticism for his comments on some vaccines, and made sweeping changes to vaccination policy, including cutting childhood immunisation recommendations by a third.

#202. Apple may try to increase revenue by doing…something with third-party AI apps

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Apple’s services business remains at record levels, but it’s facing growing pressure. The central part of that business, Apple’s App Store, has faced regulatory pressure and a slump in mobile gaming, which have hurt services growth.

However, on Monday’s earnings call, Apple signaled that it may look to boost services revenue through third-party apps. What does that mean, exactly? Unfortunately, it’s not entirely clear.

When discussing how AI could potentially “contribute to revenue growth over time,” Kevan Parekh, Apple’s chief financial officer, said: “On the product side, you know, we feel AI offers a compelling reason for customers to upgrade and switch in order to get the latest capabilities, and we also feel there’s opportunities across third-party apps and partnerships as well as first-party opportunities.”

Parekh did not elaborate on what sorts of opportunities might be available via third-party apps. However, Reuters reported Monday that investors are wondering whether Apple could profit by distributing third-party AI agents through the App Store and taking a cut of the revenue they generate.

Regulatory and legal changes have cut into App Store commission revenue in some markets, even as Apple’s services business as a whole continues to grow. Third-party estimates in August, for example, suggested the company had seen an 18% drop in U.S. commission revenue, with declines also in Brazil and Japan after those countries introduced new regulations. Apple has traditionally controlled payments within its app marketplace tightly, taking a cut of all App Store transactions. But a lawsuit by Epic Games ultimately forced the company to allow apps to redirect users to outside payment systems. Other legal challenges throughout the world have further hampered the business.

During the earnings call, Parekh referenced Apple’s App Store woes and returned to the idea that the company’s service business could expand into new territory.

“We’ve also seen some factors that impacted the performance of the App Store, and during the September quarter, the performance on the App Store was similar to the prior year, and we had a record number of paid subscriptions with double-digit growth for third-party apps on the App Store,” Parekh said. “At the same time, we did see some headwinds in mobile gaming that we’ve talked about before, and expect to see those continue into the summer quarter.”

Parekh added: “As we move ahead, we expect services growth to to be between 8 to 9% [in the December quarter]. This outlook contemplates our best estimates on sequential headwinds, primarily from third-party licensing and the additional half percentage point of foreign exchange I just mentioned.”

TechCrunch has reached out to Apple for more information and will update this story if it responds.

#201. Google takes Dreambeans to India in bid to bring AI-generated personal stories to the masses

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Google has launched its experimental AI app Dreambeans in India, its first market outside the U.S., as the company looks to tap the world’s second-largest internet market to bring personalized visual stories drawn from users’ digital activity to a broader audience.

On Monday, Google expanded Dreambeans, available on Android and iOS, to India with support for 13 local languages, including Hindi, Punjabi, Tamil, Telugu, and Bengali, alongside English. The launch comes less than two months after Google made the app available to all eligible U.S. users aged 18 and older.

Introduced in June, Google’s Dreambeans uses information from connected Google services, including Calendar, Gemini, Gmail, Photos, Search, and YouTube, to proactively generate personalized, illustrated stories about users’ interests and everyday lives, without requiring them to enter a prompt. The app initially featured static, AI-generated illustrations, but Google recently added a feature that uses its Veo video-generation model to even animate images in select stories.

Google said it chose India as Dreambeans’ first international market because of strong interest from Indian users seeking access to the app and the widespread use of its services in the country. India stood out for its high overlap of users across Gemini, Gmail, and Google Photos, Google product manager Gozde Oznur said during a press briefing.

Dreambeans has recorded nearly 55,000 downloads in the U.S. since its June debut, according to estimates from market intelligence firm Sensor Tower shared with TechCrunch. About 63% of those downloads came in September, when Google opened the app to all eligible U.S. users aged 18 and older. However, downloads dropped 84% month over month to about 5,500 in October.

In the first 30 days after Google opened Dreambeans to all eligible U.S. users, the app recorded an estimated 29,000 downloads, compared with 7.4 million for Meta’s Muse, 2.8 million for ChatGPT, 362,000 for Grok Bot, and 155,000 for Claude during their respective first 30 days after launch, Sensor Tower estimates show. However, unlike those general-purpose AI apps, Dreambeans focuses on generating personalized stories instead of responding to a broad range of user queries.

Even though Dreambeans supports many widely spoken Indian languages, users can select only one language at a time for their generated stories. The app can draw on information across Google services in multiple languages, but it cannot currently generate stories that mix languages, Oznur said during the briefing.

That could limit Dreambeans’ appeal in India, where many people regularly switch between languages, including Hindi and English, often mixing the two in what is commonly known as Hinglish.

Users can choose which Google services to connect to Dreambeans and disconnect them at any time, Oznur said. They can also delete their Dreambeans data from within the app. Moreover, Oznur asserted that information deleted from the underlying connected services is also removed from Dreambeans’ personalization system.

Users, Oznur said, can tell Dreambeans to avoid certain subjects, including business-related information, and the app will try to adjust its stories accordingly. She added that Dreambeans is available only to personal Gmail account holders, not enterprise users, and that generated stories are visible only to the account holder.

Dreambeans’ proactive approach also carries the risk of surfacing memories that users may find upsetting. Oznur acknowledged that Dreambeans could generate stories involving people who have died, for instance. She said users can tell the app to stop showing certain subjects, comparing the feature to controls in Google Photos that let users limit which memories appear.

Oznur told reporters that Google is exploring ways to make Dreambeans more actionable, moving beyond personalized stories and recommendations to help users act on the app’s suggestions.

#200. Americans head to polls in midterm elections seen as referendum on Trump

Control of the Senate and House of Representatives, both currently held by the Republicans, is at stake

Americans are heading to the polls today in US midterm elections widely seen as a referendum on Donald Trump’s second presidency and a chance for opponents to rein in his untrammeled power.

Democrats and Republicans have scrambled in recent days to deliver their final pitches to voters with control of the US Senate and House of Representatives – both currently held by the Republicans – at stake.

The campaign has been overshadowed by Trump, who has staged several dozen rallies across the country while urging his make America great again (Maga) base to vote for Republican candidates and pretend his name was on the ballot.

The party holding the White House typically loses congressional seats at midterm elections and Democrats are hoping Trump’s nose-diving popularity can be exploited to generate a “blue wave” groundswell of popular opposition that will win them both the Senate and the House.

They are seeking to wrest control over both chambers of Congress to put a brake on Trump’s presidential power, which has gone largely unchecked as he has launched a fierce immigration crackdown involving mass arrests and deportations, waged war on Iran, imposed tariffs on foreign imports , and embarked on vainglorious building projects, including a White House ballroom.

Opinion surveys have suggested the Democrats are well-positioned to win the House. The party faces a tougher task to regain control of the Senate, although it has targeted several Republican-held seats including Ohio, Texas and Maine.

A final set of polls from the New York Times and Siena University, published on Sunday, put Democratic candidates ahead in five of seven key battleground contests – including Texas and Kansas, where voters have not sent a Democrat to the Senate since 1988 and 1932, respectively.

The final stretch of the campaign was not without drama for the Democrats, however. In Michigan – widely seen as a crucial pillar to either party’s ambitions of control in the Senate – Democratic congresswoman Haley Stevens, who lost her party’s senate primary to Abdul El-Sayed, endorsed his Republican rival, Mike Rogers, on Sunday.

Stevens was widely condemned by fellow Democrats over the move, which followed a feud with the El-Sayed campaign over an outside group’s campaign ad that blamed the high cost of living on US support for Israel. Chuck Schumer, the Senate minority leader, described her endorsement of Rogers as “a despicable betrayal”.

“Whatever they’re trying to distract you, it’s because they’re picking your pocket,” El Sayed told hundreds of supporters in Royal Oak, Michigan on Monday.

The extraordinary rupture in Michigan took places days after the party faced damaging headlines in Alaska, where it was reported that the Democratic senate candidate’s campaign was behind an effort to recruit a challenger with almost the same name as her rival, the Republican senator Dan Sullivan. (The Democratic candidate, Mary Peltola, has denied any knowledge of such a scheme on the part of her campaign.)

Before the report of the Peltola campaign’s involvement, the Department of Justice (DoJ) filed a lawsuit to block the certification of Alaska’s Senate results in the event of a Republican defeat, citing potential “voter confusion” over the presence of two Dan Sullivans on the ballot. The lawsuit has since been dismissed by a judge.

Trump has put himself center stage by crisscrossing the country to deliver a succession of speeches for Republican candidate, despite opinion polls showing his approval ratings falling to record lows of between 33 and 37%.

Democrats have sought to reinforce the message that the election is about Trump while trying to highlight the perceived corruption of the US president and his family, along with affordability issues, and the impact of the conflict with Iran, which is seen as having exacerbated the cost of living.

Opinion surveys have shown that voters have become increasingly concerned about corruption in government. A recent Harris poll for the Guardian found that 76% think elected officials care more about enriching themselves than improving the economy for ordinary people.

Disclosures to the US office of government ethics show that Trump has earned about $2.2bn since returning to the White House, including royalties from his own name-branded meme coin.

As the president has grown wealthier, voters have chafed under rising living costs, which Trump vowed to reduce during his 2024 presidential campaign.

The war with Iran – which Trump launched last February in tandem with Israel – has stoked inflation by increasing global oil prices, which has led to higher prices at the pump for American motorists and raised the cost of diesel, a vital fuel for supply chains and farmers.

The US elections also take place under a shadow of uncertainty about whether Trump will accept the results if they do not go the Republicans’ way.

Alaska is just one of the cases pursued by the justice department – under the control of Trump’s close ally, Todd Blanche, the attorney general – that opponents say are designed to cast doubt on the election’s integrity, including through baseless claims of mass voting by non-US citizens.

The justice department plans to deploy a record 1,000 election monitors to polling stations across the US. Critics have denounced the move as voter intimidation.

#199. Apple Watch’s AI features for rewinding and recapping conversations begin beta testing

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Apple’s AI-powered “Audio Intelligence” technology for the Apple Watch is now rolling out into beta testing, initially for developers in the iOS 27.3 update, after first being announced at the company’s Surprise and Shine hardware event in September. The technology includes a set of four opt-in AI features that allow Apple Watch Series 12 wearers to detect important sounds, identify songs, catch up on conversations, and take notes.

The sound recognition feature will help deaf and hard-of-hearing users identify important sounds like sirens, alarms, or doorbells from their watch, while the song recognition feature taps into Apple’s Shazam app to detect music playing in the background and identify the song name and artist.

However, the more potentially controversial features are those that listen to human conversations: Live Rewind and Siri Recaps.

With Live Rewind, the idea is to let you go back in time to see what someone just said, in case you missed it. For instance, if someone was giving you verbal directions or reading a long list of specials at a restaurant, you could double-press on the Watch’s digital crown to see what was said in the last 15 seconds as a text snippet displayed on the watch face. You can either view this snippet and then do nothing, and it will disappear permanently. Or you can save it to the standalone Siri app that shipped with iOS 27 for later access, which might be helpful if you’re trying to remember someone’s name or take a quick note from a fast-paced presentation.

Apple says that users will also be able to tap an “Ask Siri” option that appears below the text snippet if they have questions about something that was said — like a technical term they don’t understand, or perhaps some sauce option the waiter mentioned that they’re unfamiliar with, among other things.

When you double-press the digital crown, an audible chime appears and an animation displays on the watch. This is meant to signal to people through the combination of the gesture, sound, and the visual display that you’ve recorded something that was said.

These snippets will be deleted automatically after 30 seconds if you don’t save them.

The other option, Siri Recaps, takes this ambient listening technology even further by allowing Apple Watch owners to take high-level notes of their conversations.

Importantly, Apple says it’s not identifying speakers, saving the raw audio, or providing word-for-word transcriptions. Instead, the feature summarizes a conversation with a short sentence and some bullet points that identify key points. For instance, depending on the topic, the bullet points might include things like important dates or deadlines, pricing or payment plan information, and other factual information.

Apple suggested the feature could be used in scenarios like work meetings, parent-teacher conferences, personal training sessions, or other places where you may need to remember what tasks you have to complete or things that were said. Essentially, it’s supposed to mimic a user’s ability to jot down their own notes — without adding emotion or tone.

This could be helpful particularly at times when you aren’t able to write up your own notes, either because your hands are full or you’re trying to concentrate on whatever’s being said at the time. The recaps won’t record conversations when you’re listening to music with your headphones or using their noise cancellation features.

At the end of the day, you’re prompted to review your saved Siri Recaps when the feature is enabled, and then choose which, if any, you want to save. These notes can be saved to your Notes app or even exported to your Journal app, if they’re more personal in nature. If you do nothing, the recaps will delete themselves within seven days.

This reminder to review the Siri Recaps you’ve recorded also appears when you first unlock your Apple Watch in the morning, too.

Users can opt to enable the Siri Recaps manually, or they can configure a schedule, such as only having the recapping take place while at work, or making sure that they never run at night. Even if they’re running on a schedule, you can still turn off the recapping feature at any time from the Control Center.

Apple notes that Siri Recaps has been designed to omit sensitive information like government-assigned identifiers, financial and authentication data, certain personal identifiers, and any language that would promote self-harm.

The Audio Intelligence features for the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4 rely on a combination of Apple’s hardware, software, and services. This includes the S11 chip, built-in microphone, and the Apple Intelligence models that run on the iPhone, as well as Apple’s Private Cloud Compute.

The always-on processor includes a secure enclave — a hardware-protected memory space that can’t be accessed by the rest of the operating system — where a dedicated audio buffer processes the raw audio. This allows the audio to be processed without it actually being accessible to anyone, including Apple. The audio is only accessible to the paired iPhone through a secure pairing mechanism between it and the watch.

To work, lightweight AI models on the S11 chip on the Apple Watch detect if there’s a conversation happening that the wearer is a part of, which is then transferred to the secure enclave on the iPhone. On-device processing on the iPhone turns the audio into condensed text, meaning the text of the conversation with the filler words removed. This condensed text is then encrypted and sent to Apple’s Private Cloud Compute (PCC) to create the recap of the conversation that is sent back to the phone. (The recap and any summarizations used to create it are deleted from PCC immediately afterwards).

The recaps you can then access on your iPhone are only accessible to you, not to Apple. And they’re encrypted when they’re synced between your multiple Apple devices.

Similarly, Apple Watch transfers audio to the iPhone for processing to create the 15-second snippets for Live Rewind.

Apple says none of these features should impact the battery life of your watch or phone. The features will later roll out to the public beta for broader testing before becoming publicly available to all.

#197. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.
