#127. French officials eye social media’s role in promoting unrest.

The interior ministry estimated that 320,000 people had demonstrated nationwide, a smaller number than the previous two Saturdays. The police and protesters clashed in Paris and other cities.

Hundreds of thousands of protesters took to the streets in cities across France again on Saturday, the latest expression of national grievance that has led to clashes between protesters and the police and kept the country on edge.

As dusk fell and official rallies wound down, unauthorized marches splintered off in Paris, stoking fresh fears of chaos as night approached.

Adding to those fears: Halloween, which falls on Saturday. The French authorities, citing calls on social media for people to revolt that encouraged them to wear masks and costumes, warned that the risk of a new outbreak of violence was high.

In Paris, tensions began flaring between protesters and the police in the late afternoon, as officers fired tear gas at people who refused to disperse. The Paris police estimated that 70,000 people marched in the demonstration, compared with 110,000 last Saturday. After the main demonstration ended, hundreds of protesters, many masked and clad in black, formed an unauthorized procession in eastern Paris.

The interior ministry estimated that nationwide, 320,000 people demonstrated, compared with 410,000 last week. Protesters and the police also clashed in Marseille, Lyon, Toulouse, and Rennes.

Corentin Schnell, a 19-year-old engineering student, took part in the unauthorized march in Paris because, he said, he was angered by accounts of the police using tear gas and stun grenades against protesters in past weeks. “I’ve come to demonstrate and to try to provide medical care — to limit the damage, regardless of which side it’s on,” he said.

Earlier in the week, France’s interior minister, Laurent Nuñez, promised to deploy 70,000 police officers, matching the numbers on Oct. 24. That operation helped keep a lid on outbreaks of arson and looting, although then, too, there were clashes as the police tried to disperse the crowds in the evening.

There was ample fuel for a repeat of those combustible scenes. Students, who are returning to school on Monday after a two-week break, were not mollified by the government’s initial response to their demands. The Education Ministry agreed to cap some class sizes, offer low-interest loans to rebuild school buildings and phase out an unpopular online college admissions platform.

The students vowed to go on protesting. Their two-month-old movement has grown to include university students, public workers and veterans of the “yellow vest” movement that convulsed France from 2018 to 2020.

The authorities have said they are most worried about trouble breaking out after nightfall.

“We are seeing numerous calls to gather coming from the far left, the radicalized extreme left, and from certain radicalized ‘yellow vests’ websites, which are urging people to attend rallies or public gatherings starting at 8 p.m. in many cities across France,” Mr. Nuñez said at a news conference.

Some of the messages were violent, Mr. Nuñez said, calling for “revolution” and “insurrection.” Those gatherings will be forbidden, and any attempt to gather despite the ban will lead to arrests, he said.

Demonstrators have been encouraged on social media to turn up wearing Halloween costumes. Mr. Nuñez warned that it was a criminal offense to conceal one’s face during a demonstration that disrupted public order.

Social media: French officials are increasingly concerned about social media’s role in attracting people and inciting violent behavior. Speculation that the government considered temporarily suspending several popular sites drew a denial from President Emmanuel Macron himself. Read more ›

Far-right activists: While the government has blamed far-left activists for much of the mayhem, officials confirmed that bands of young men with links to the far right have also infiltrated the demonstrations. They use the pretext of protecting shop owners but often clash with far-left groups.

Widening movement: The nationwide unrest in France began with a handful of student demonstrations at high schools in suburban Paris last month. Read more ›

The authorized demonstration in Paris has come to an end, with some clashes with the police. In its place, a spontaneous procession of several hundred protesters took shape along Avenue Daumesnil in eastern Paris. Many were dressed in black and had their faces covered. Part of the crowd was singing and dancing to songs blasted on speakers. As the crowd got closer to a square with bars and restaurants full of customers, some waiters hurried to close outdoor seating areas as police officers arrived on motorcycles. Even with the heavy police presence, no clashes erupted and the protesters marched into the night.

Among the protesters in the unauthorized demonstration were Morgane Voisin, 22, and Corentin Schnell, 19, both of whom were wearing white outfits indicating that they were “street medics,” or those able to deliver first aid. Ms. Voisin, a sales consultant, said she came to demonstrate because her younger siblings had faced harsh conditions in high school. Mr. Schnell, an engineering student, said he was prompted to join when his little brother told him about getting tear-gassed and almost hit by a stun grenade at a protest earlier this month. “The mere fact that people are being injured by the police makes me furious,” Mr. Schnell said. “So I’ve come to demonstrate and to try to provide medical care — to limit the damage, regardless of which side it’s on.” On Saturday, the pair said they saw one person who had been injured in the head, but aid arrived quickly. “Long live youth!” Ms. Voisin said.

I can hear loud bangs at the end of the demonstration route and I can see many people running backward, as well as tear gas in the distance. Some protesters are throwing mortar-like fireworks.

The mood at the Paris protest has definitely changed. Though I can still hear some protesters singing, the protest has turned violent.

Police officers are now blocking many of the roads in the neighborhood at the end of the demonstration route. I saw a young woman surrounded by medics — she was bleeding from the face. I saw flames at the end of one avenue. I also saw police officers charge toward the crowd. Some residents are trying to make their way back home quickly, covering their faces to avoid tear gas.

Tensions in Paris continue to rise. Police are skirmishing with 200 to 300 rioters who have refused to disperse at the end of a rally. The Paris police said the rioters threw Molotov cocktails and fireworks at officers. A car was set afire.

Tensions are now flaring between protesters and the police in Paris. Police are firing rounds of tear gas at people, who are refusing to disperse. The Paris police estimated that 70,000 people marched in the demonstration, compared with 110,000 last Saturday.

The Interior Ministry estimated that 320,000 people took part in protests across the country today. That is somewhat fewer than last week (410,000) and the week before (550,000).

The demonstration in Paris remains peaceful, even festive. But police and protesters have clashed in Marseille, Lyon, Toulouse, and Rennes. Tear gas and water cannons have been deployed as the police try to disperse crowds. Violence typically breaks out at the end of these rallies, when people refuse to leave the scene.

I am walking along the authorized route for the demonstration in Paris. For the most part, the mood has been festive. Someone even dropped candy from his balcony, drawing cheers from the crowd.

After, I watched a half-dozen protesters dressed in black and wearing masks push trash bins in the middle of the crowd. One was already burning as they made their way, and they set the second on fire not far from where a police unit was stationed. Other demonstrators used water to put out the fire, causing a small clash with the ones wearing in black. “That’s no way to protest,” one of the demonstrators who put out the fire said.

The protest in Paris has a joyous and playful atmosphere. Brass bands are playing music, and many protesters have come dressed in Halloween costumes. Many said they wanted to help reverse the narrative set on Oct. 17 when riots spilled out of marches across France, leading to destruction and looting.

“I’m really worried about how the riots have been instrumentalized by the far right,” said Helen Olive, 50, who was among a group of parents who came dressed as “super parents.” “We want to turn that narrative around.”

Literature student Arthur Fleury, 20, arrived as a prisoner riding a dinosaur and wore a mask of the far-right politician Marine Le Pen. “Protests are about making demands but also having fun,” he said. “I was going for an absurd costume to capture the spirit of the movement.”

Early estimates from the French interior ministry suggest crowds are smaller today than the past two Saturdays. At 2 p.m., the ministry estimated that 15,000 people were taking part in 65 protests. That compares to 37,000 people last week at that time and 67,000 on Oct. 17. These numbers can be expected to grow as the day progresses. The ministry said it had arrested 27 people, compared with 217 by this time last Saturday. Protest organizers have yet to issue a crowd estimate; their numbers are typically far higher.

I’m at the Place de la Bastille. It is normally a bustling square, particularly on a beautiful, sunny Saturday. Now, police officers with shields and helmets patrol in groups. A loudspeaker blares a warning that gatherings and protests are forbidden to take place here by a police decree and that anyone who breaks it will be fined 135 euros.

Union and student organizers had again hoped to start their march here today, and again it was denied by the police and moved to the eastern edge of the city.

As France prepares for another round of protests, officials have grown increasingly concerned about the role played by social media in attracting people to demonstrations and inciting them to violent behavior.

Speculation that the government considered temporarily suspending several popular sites drew a denial from President Emmanuel Macron himself.

But in an interview on YouTube, Mr. Macron said that social media platforms would be required “to remove illegal content, and to be very vigilant about the use of certain features to prevent them from being misused by people who want to engage in looting or other such activities.”

That followed a report by a left-leaning online news site that Prime Minister Sébastien Lecornu had sent a letter to leading telecommunications operators, asking them to be prepared to block Snapchat, TikTok and Instagram. Mr. Lecornu’s office declined to comment on the authenticity of the letter.

Of the four reported recipients, one did not respond to questions and two declined to comment. A spokesman for the fourth, Orange, the only one in which the state is a shareholder, declined to confirm that a letter was received but said that “like any other operator, we are required to comply with the obligations legally imposed on us by the competent authorities, in accordance with applicable law.”

The nationwide unrest in France began with a handful of student demonstrations at high schools in suburban Paris last month.

Now, in cities across the country, antigovernment marchers are protesting expensive living conditions, high fuel prices and conditions in schools. Using the unrest as a smoke screen for vandalism, rioters and looters have also joined in.

With the protests in their second month, the police have arrested thousands, and politicians are divided over how to respond.

Here is a breakdown of what has happened.

Soon after returning from the summer break, students at a high school in the working class Parisian suburb of Créteil staged a protest, according to videos posted to social media on Sept. 18. The high schoolers objected to inadequate resources and high workloads.

Within days, students elsewhere around Paris initiated similar demonstrations. At schools in the capital’s poorest suburbs, students blocked entrances to protest crumbling infrastructure, overcrowded classrooms and what they say are flaws in the admissions system for higher education.

By Sept. 29, the unrest had spread to around 400 schools around the country, according to rally organizers.

The union of high school students said the blockades were a protest against a “lack of teachers and staff, dilapidated schools, overcrowded schedules and classrooms.”

Students barricaded the entrances to some schools with obstacles including e-bikes, while others threw projectiles and set fire to trash cans.

The French education minister, Édouard Geffray, said that some of the students’ demands were “legitimate” but condemned the confrontational nature of their protests. He said that students had chased teachers and targeted school principals with firework-launching tubes.

By the end of September, nearly 80 people had been injured, and the authorities had detained more than 440, including many minors.

Within days, the number of schools engulfed in the protests had doubled. According to the Interior Ministry, by Oct. 2, they had spread to more than 1,000 schools across France, including 220 that were blockaded. University students also began taking part.

Around this time, the authorities said that the protests were mutating from legitimate expressions of educational grievances into something more dangerous, accusing people of shooting mortar-style fireworks, burning public buildings and looting.

By Oct. 2, nearly 400 police officers and over 230 school employees and students had been injured. The number of arrests climbed to 3,000.

The actions widened in October to encompass labor unions, parents and other activists, as different grievances converged into a mass protest.

On Oct. 6, around 256,000 people marched through Paris and 40 other French cities, according to the Interior Ministry. A union of high school students said protesters had occupied some 1,800 high schools.

The protests were mostly peaceful, but a fire was ignited in Lyon, and police officers used tear gas, including in Paris. The total number of arrested rose to 6,000, the authorities said. A major union of French judges and prosecutors and a human rights group accused the police of using disproportionate force, including against children as young as 14.

Two days later, on Oct. 8, smaller demonstrations were staged throughout the country, rallying around 71,500 people. The following week, the Interior Ministry said that 160,000 people joined marches on Oct. 13 across France.

More than half a million people joined 110 antigovernment marches throughout France on Oct. 17, according to an official estimate. A major union, the C.G.T., put the number even higher, at 800,000. Using either count, it was the largest day of protest since the demonstrations began in September.

Many of the marchers were peaceful, but some were not. Around the country, rioters vandalized around 1,500 shops, the authorities said. In Paris, where over 100,000 people marched peacefully, a few hundred rioters faced off against officers. According to the police, the rioters burned cars and smashed storefronts.

In the large town of Limoges, in central France, rioters set fire to the 19th-century City Hall. In a moment captured on video, the building’s spire collapsed in flames.

#126. French pupils resume school blockades as country braces for more protests

Minister warns weeks without lessons are putting exams at risk as unions plan nationwide strike on Thursday

Dozens of high schools across France were blockaded again on Monday as the government braced for another tense week of protests over school conditions and the high cost of living.

The protests come amid warnings that weeks without lessons are putting some pupils’ school year and final exams at risk.

As children returned after the two-week school holiday, students’ unions ignored Emmanuel Macron’s call to end the lycée blockades, which have been running since September and have prompted one of the biggest protest movements and social crises of his presidency.

From early morning in Paris and its suburbs, as well as in cities including Lille, Toulouse and Marseille, schoolchildren demanding more replacement teachers and repairs to buildings barricaded school entrances with wheelie bins. Police officers outside some schools attempted to drag the bins away and made arrests.

University students’ unions were planning additional barricades at faculties on Tuesday. Trade unions have called for a large-scale strike on Thursday, with rail workers, teachers and private-sector employees planning to join demonstrations in cities across France to support schoolchildren’s demands for better conditions and protest against the rising cost of living and low salaries.

Nathanaël Combary from the school students’ union Renouveau Lycéen, who is also a member of Macron’s centrist party Renaissance, said schoolchildren must keep going in their “battle of strength” with the government to push for more funding, better access to higher education and repairs to decrepit schools.

He said measures offered by the government last week to cap some class sizes and review the university applications platform were inadequate. Combary told BFMTV that protests were the only way for schoolchildren to push a government response. He said: “Democratically speaking it’s a bit scandalous that we have to reach this type of situation to be heard but it’s the only solution we have.”

The education minister, Édouard Geffray, said he feared the school year was now “at risk” for many students in their final years who had missed weeks of classes because of the blockades and would be unable to complete coursework or catch up on the curriculum for exams.

He said: “We have pupils who are sitting their baccalauréat at the end of the year who for hundreds of thousands of them have had no classes for four or five weeks if you include holidays, and for whom the school year is now at risk. And that’s something that we can’t allow.”

Geffray said the school blockades were “illegal” and his priority was for children to return to class.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

In Montpellier, the Socialist senator Hussein Bourgi accused the government of “misconduct” and “provocation” after video footage showed a 53-year-old school nurse saying something to police near a school barricade before being forced face-down to the ground, handcuffed and taken into police custody. The local prefect, in charge of police operations, defended the arrest, saying the woman had insulted officers and resisted police.

The education ministry said 41 high schools were blocked across France – around 1% of the nationwide total. Students’ unions put the figure at 150.

Nicolas Bonnet, of the headteachers’ union SNPDEN-UNSA, said: “It’s time for us to return to our job: teaching and supporting pupils.” He warned that coursework, final-year exams and university applications were at risk.

He said he understood why high-school students felt the government had not offered enough to fix problems in schools, but urged more dialogue between the government and pupils.

The prime minister, Sébastien Lecornu, said he would meet school students’ unions again next week.

#124. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#122. Who is hacking Europe’s supermarket fridges and freezers, and why?

After a second apparent cyber-attack in weeks, we examine possible culprits and their motives

Shop fridges and freezers across Europe hit by fresh suspected cyber-attack

Supermarket fridges and freezers in seven European countries have malfunctioned, their thermostats remotely cranked up in some cases to 50C (122F).

The EU has condemned what it said was “a deliberate attempt to disrupt our food supply chain and undermine consumers’ confidence in it”.

The attack hit fridges in Belgian branches of Carrefour, Intermarché and Ikea, Coop and ICA in Sweden, Delhaize in Luxembourg, Kesko in Finland, SuperValu in Ireland and shops in Estonia.

It appeared to be a reprise of attacks three weeks ago when French retailers had to ditch huge amounts of food when their fridges suddenly overheated as a result of anonymous hackers seizing control of their internet-exposed appliance management software.

Something similar may have happened in the US in early August when a series of mysterious fridge malfunctions hit commissaries at least six military bases. The Pentagon is investigating a suspected hack.

This is feared to signal a new front in cyber-attack and hacking tactics. Fridges are not unique in having their controls exposed to hackers. Many ovens, washing machines, dishwashers and central heating systems are linked to the internet to allow remote access and automated control.

No politically motivated “hacktivist” group has claimed responsibility and neither has a ransomware gang exploited the vulnerability to demand the supermarkets pay – which in any event would be difficult because the damage is already done when their fridges overheat.

“The two most plausible attribution scenarios for me are some kind of state-based signalling and demonstration of capability or some kind of lone-wolf hacker that is conducting these attacks for their own personal reasons,” said Rafe Pilling, the senior director for threat intelligence at the cybersecurity company Sophos.

The state-linked actor theory seems most likely, in part because of the obvious psychological impact on large populations of seeing food stripped from shelves. North Korea is believed to have been behind last week’s Binance cryptocurrency heist, but in this case hackers linked to Russia and China are also likely to be in the frame.

Western intelligence chiefs say Moscow is waging a campaign of sabotage in Europe that takes many different forms and is likely to intensify in the coming months.

Discerning the culprit is made harder by the relative simplicity of the hack. No malware was needed, which means there is no trace of a weapon at the scene of the crime. Pilling compared it to “wearing rubber gloves and messing with the switches”.

The software that manages commercial fridges is connected to the internet, which means lines of code can be left vulnerable to hackers. Unlike desktop or mobile devices, on which users and IT managers are often alerted to the need to install updates that contain patches to repair constantly emerging vulnerabilities, the software in items such as fridges can be left for long periods without updates.

Nick Jackson, the director of cyber-operations at the cybersecurity company Bitdefender, said one reason companies might move slowly to install patches is they can take time to run, which leaves periods when machinery needs to be offline – an obvious problem for food retailers. But he said a simpler explanation could be that the people running the fridges are not aware of the problem, “don’t think about that and it’s not top of their agenda”.

In Monday’s wave of incidents, Coop in Sweden said some of its fridges were linked to a 4G mobile network that made them more exposed. ICA said its system was provided by Danfoss, the Danish company that had problems in France.

The simple answer is to patch their software systems. According to the Belgian refrigerator group Sabcobel, the impact of Monday’s hack could have been far worse if they had not started patching fridges after the 12 October attack in France.

In the UK, which has not yet been affected, retailers are “currently working with the National Cyber Security Centre(NSCC) to ensure their fridges and freezers are secure as possible against potential attacks” said Graham Wynn, the assistant director of regulatory affairs at the British Retail Consortium.

Internet-exposed refrigeration systems are most popular in countries including France, Italy, Greece and Belgium, according to the cybersecurity company Forescout.

Daniel dos Santos, its vice president of research, said: “When we checked the data for these exposed systems online of supermarkets, the UK is not in the top 10.” He said the risk was not negligible, but “I wouldn’t say it’s super-high right now for the UK specifically”.

A spokesperson for the NCSC said it was “working with its international partners and industry to fully understand details of an incident”.

#120. North Korea denies role in record Binance hack, calls US charge 'provocation'

Kim Yo Jong dismissed FBI claims that North Korean hackers stole more than $6 billion from Binance, as the exchange began reopening withdrawals on more networks.

North Korea has hit back at US accusations that its hackers drained more than $6 billion (more than 5.36 billion euros) from cryptocurrency exchange Binance. Kim Yo Jong, sister of leader Kim Jong Un, called the charge "a grave political provocation," according to state media agency KCNA.

Kim described the US claim as "absurd rhetoric and nothing new.”

The accusation came from the FBI, which blamed a state-tied hacking group called TraderTraitor over the weekend.

This was reportedly the biggest crypto-exchange heist on record, well above the roughly $1.5 billion Bybit raid in 2025, which was also attributed to North Korean hackers. TRM Labs linked the attack to the same group and said it shifted most of the money out within 20 minutes, German news agency DPA reported.

Kim Yo Jong said Pyongyang now had "issues to reconsider" because of Washington's hostility.

North Korea has so far stayed silent on Trump's overtures about reviving summit talks. Analysts in Seoul told Reuters news agency the speed of the denial points to concern that Washington could step up efforts to cut off a funding source widely believed to bankroll arms development.

Separately, Binance announced Monday that withdrawals will restart for additional assets across several blockchains, among them Binance Smart Chain, Ethereum, Solana, XRP and Sui, from 04:00 GMT Tuesday.

Selected tokens on Arbitrum, Optimism, Tron and Base are part of the same rollout.

Binance said it replaced most of the stolen assets out of its own funds.

#119. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#118. Russian bombing of Ukrainian aid centre kills at least 26, Kyiv says

Ukrainian president Zelenskyy says attack on aid hub was ‘vile’ and shows Russia does not want to de-escalate

Russia on Monday bombed an aid distribution centre and neighbouring shelter in southern Ukraine, killing at least 26 ​people, Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy and the country’s emergency services said.

Emergency services said two members of a rescue crew and four police officers were among the dead in the village ⁠of Komyshuvakha, in south-eastern Zaporizhzhia region. Five people were being treated in hospital, including two rescue workers.

Zelenskyy, in his nightly video address, said Russian forces initially dropped bombs on a school in the village that was used as a shelter and for ⁠distributing humanitarian assistance.

There were no classes at the school, he said, as the village, lying south-east of the region’s main city, ​also called Zaporizhzhia, was too close to the frontline. ‌

“The Russians struck first ‌just as people were arriving – a few aerial bombs,” he said. “Then there was a second strike – more air bombs, this time on ‌the shelter. A vile terrorist strike.”

As night fell in Komyshuvakha, emergency responders used torches to search through the rubble of collapsed buildings, according to photos and video posted online by local officials. Regina Kharchenko, secretary of the Zaporizhzhia city council, said that 27 people were missing in the attack.

Efforts to rescue people Monday evening were hindered as Russian drones launched additional attacks in the area that had been bombed, according to Mykhailo Semikin, a military official in Zaporizhzhia.

Zelenskyy said the Russians could not have been unaware that residents remaining in Komyshuvakha gathered each week to pick up humanitarian packages from the site.

The attack, he said, had shown that Moscow was intent on killing people instead of building on the past week’s ceasefire in the embattled southern city of Oleshky, brokered by US first lady Melania Trump.

“Russians continue to demonstrate, time and again, their hatred of Ukraine and everything that exists here,” he said.

“If Russia genuinely ‌wanted de-escalation, there was an opportunity following the situation in Oleshky to continue taking humanitarian steps. We proposed this. But Russia continues to kill people.”

The ceasefire last week enabled an evacuation of the Russian-occupied Oleshky.

It was the longest pause in fighting between the neighbours in the war, now in its fifth year, which has killed hundreds of thousands of people and displaced millions.

The Kremlin said that no other similar missions were being planned “at the moment”.

US-led talks between Russia and Ukraine are set to resume next week, US special envoy Steve Witkoff said on Monday.

President Donald Trump has pushed for a settlement to the dragging war but there has been no breakthrough.

The talks – to be held 10 November and 11 in Abu Dhabi, Witkoff said – come as Kyiv and its European allies worry that Trump has increasingly sided with Moscow.

Here is a roundup of the biggest headlines from across India on Tuesday, November 3.

Severe cyclonic storm 'Murasu' began its landfall close to Ongole in the Prakasam district of the coastal state of Andhra Pradesh during the early hours on Tuesday.

The cyclone, which, according to the Indian Meteorological Department (IMD), is currently moving towards the north-northwest, has left a trail of destruction in the region.

Heavy spells of rainfall were also likely across south coastal Andhra Pradesh during the next 12 hours, IMD said.

The cyclone is currently over the west central Bay of Bengal close to the south Andhra Pradesh coast and is likely to weaken gradually into a cyclonic storm by noon and further into a deep depression by Tuesday midnight.

#114. Denmark pushes for EU ban on ‘turbo chickens’ as secret footage causes outrage

Minister says government will work towards banning fast-growing breed across country and throughout EU

The Danish government is pursuing a national ban on “turbo chickens” and is calling for the fast-growing breed to be prohibited across Europe.

The move comes amid widespread outrage over allegations of lawbreaking by one of Denmark’s big chicken producers.

According to a TV2 documentary broadcast last week, producer Christian Tvergaard broke Danish law by giving chickens only half the amount of darkness per day that is required, in order to encourage them to eat more. The documentary featured secretly filmed footage from a chicken coop in North Jutland.

The Ross 308 breed, labelled the turbo chicken, is the most widespread in Danish poultry production. It grows from about 50 grams to 2kg in just 33 days. Such rapid growth can lead to problems with the animal’s bones and organs. Other chicken breeds take between 46 and 63 days to reach the same weight.

Denmark’s minister for nature and animal welfare, Christian Rabjerg Madsen, said he discouraged Danes from buying turbo chickens, telling the broadcaster: “I want to work for a national ban. But it’s clear that this is something that needs to be investigated both legally and economically.”

He added: “Now we will work for a national ban, we will work for a ban at EU level.”

In response to the documentary, Tvergaard has admitted to making some wrong decisions.

He told TV2: “I look inward and must admit that I have made some wrong decisions. There is no excuse for that, but I can learn from it and correct where I have not done well enough.

“And I have done that, both when it comes to the lighting programme and a number of the workflows we have in the stable, where both procedures and the roster have been tightened up.”

The proposal for the ban on turbo chickens is gaining political momentum and attracting cross-party support in Denmark. But industry figures warn that to be effective, any ban must include imported chickens as well.

Klement Hinkbøl Ribergaard, the chief executive of chicken producer Danpo, said: “If Denmark alone bans the production of fast-growing chickens while shops, restaurants and consumers in the Danish market can continue to buy imported, fast-growing chicken of more or less questionable quality, we risk a situation in which animal welfare certainly does not benefit.”

With fast-growing breeds so prominent across the EU, it would mark a landmark shift. According to Eurogroup for Animals, 95% of chickens in the bloc are now raised in intensive systems and bred to produce the cheapest amount of meat possible, creating a “race to the bottom”.

“Fast-growing breeds are inherently predisposed to suffering, with their bodies growing so quickly they can barely stand on their legs,” said Reineke Hameleers, the advocacy group’s chief executive. “Denmark’s political willingness to stop this is an example that should be followed by the EU, for it presents the progressiveness this industry needs, one that is backed up by scientific evidence.”

Britta Riis, chief executive of Animal Protection Denmark, said turbo chickens were the result of a “deeply unethical and grotesque form of breeding” that led to “systematic abuse” and considerable suffering for the birds. The organisation is calling for all chickens to be bred for the same amount of time as organic breeds, which is 56 days.

Animal welfare has featured heavily in public debate in recent months in Denmark. The issue of pig farming dominated the last general election to such an extent that it was nicknamed the “pig election”.

The country is due to stop mink farming by August 2029 after the current government decided not to seek an EU exemption on the practice.

#112. India's opposition holds mega rally seeking election chief's resignation

Opposition leader Rahul Gandhi led Sunday's protest

Tens of thousands gathered in Delhi for a rally demanding the resignation of India's chief election commissioner over a controversial revision of voter lists.

Organised by the opposition INDIA bloc and led by Rahul Gandhi's Congress party, the protest drew many of the alliance's most prominent leaders in a show of strength against Prime Minister Narendra Modi's government.

Protesters waved flags and chanted "Save the vote, save the country" and "Vote thief, give up power".

It was the latest in a series of protests over electoral roll revision that the opposition says has removed eligible voters to benefit the Bharatiya Janata Party. The BJP and the Election Commission deny the allegations.

The crowds gathered at Ramlila Maidan, a sprawling ground in central Delhi known for hosting major political and cultural events. Some protesters sang and danced to patriotic Bollywood songs, while others waited beneath a large canopy in the colours of the Indian flag to hear opposition leaders speak.

Gandhi and several senior opposition leaders arrived at the venue in an open jeep, travelling through crowds of cheering supporters.

The event was also attended by Congress leaders Sonia Gandhi and Mallikarjun Kharge, alongside leaders of regional parties such as Mamata Banerjee, Akhilesh Yadav, Mehbooba Mufti, Supriya Sule and Tejashwi Yadav.

Addressing the crowds, Rahul Gandhi said those gathered had sent a message to Modi, Home Minister Amit Shah and Chief Election Commissioner Gyanesh Kumar.

"This is not the voice of a single political party or of politicians. It is the voice of India's youth, women, farmers and workers," he said.

"We will not allow democracy to be destroyed in India. We will not allow the constitution to be destroyed."

Other opposition leaders echoed Gandhi's criticism of the Election Commission. Congress president Kharge said the alliance would continue its campaign until Kumar resigned, while Banerjee pledged to keep fighting against what she described as threats to democracy.

Speaking to reporters in Delhi on Sunday, senior BJP leader and federal minister JP Nadda accused the opposition of trying to create unrest and destabilise the government under the guise of defending democracy.

He described the rally as a "calculated conspiracy to plunge the nation into anarchy - in the name of democracy".

Tens of thousands of people gathered at the Ramlila Maidan

At the centre of the dispute is an exercise known as the Special Intensive Revision (SIR), which has resulted in more than 130 million names being removed from India's draft electoral rolls.

The Election Commission says the exercise is intended to remove duplicate registrations and the names of people who have died, moved away or are otherwise ineligible to vote. It says eligible voters who have been wrongly excluded can seek to have their names restored.

But opposition parties allege that the process is removing legitimate voters and could benefit the governing BJP in future elections.

The controversy deepened in September when The Indian Express newspaper reported, external that Kumar had made key decisions about the revision despite repeated objections from the two other election commissioners.

The poll panel said differences of opinion were normal in any institution and did not affect its functioning.

Sunday's rally followed weeks of protests over the voter-list revision, including demonstrations by student groups and the Gen Z-led Cockroach Janta Party (CJP).

Last month, Gandhi led tens of thousands on a march from central Delhi's Connaught Place to the Supreme Court, demanding Kumar's resignation.

The CJP also backed Sunday's rally but chose not to participate. Its founder Abhijeet Dipke said the group wanted to maintain its independence from established political parties.

People from all age groups came for the event

The event took place under strict conditions imposed by Delhi Police. According to local media reports, organisers were required to end the gathering by 5pm, install CCTV cameras at the venue and ensure that protesters did not march beyond the designated grounds.

The restrictions came against a backdrop of tensions over the policing of demonstrations in the capital. On 10 October, several leaders of the CJP were detained during protests against Kumar, while BBC reporters witnessed police dragging demonstrators away and beating some with batons.

At Ramlila Maidan, however, the atmosphere was largely peaceful, with people of different ages and professions turning out to express their concerns about the integrity of India's elections.

"We don't even know if this is a people's mandate or a stolen mandate," Isha, a development sector professional from Delhi, told the BBC.

Deepro Guha, a lawyer from Delhi, said he was concerned about the way the revision had been carried out.

"It's important because voting is the basis of democracy. If people's names are removed without any explanation, there is a problem," he said.

Opposition alliance held a mock impeachment of CEC Kumar on Saturday

On Saturday, opposition leaders also staged a symbolic impeachment of Chief Election Commissioner Gyanesh Kumar, accusing him of undermining the electoral process.

The proceedings were purely symbolic and had no legal standing. Under Indian law, the chief election commissioner can only be removed through a formal parliamentary process.

But the move was another show of unity by the INDIA bloc, an alliance of opposition parties formed in 2023 to to take on Modi's BJP.

Modi did not directly comment on the mock impeachment, but at public rallies on Friday and Saturday he accused opposition parties of trying to create chaos and undermining the dignity of the constitution.

#111. Peacock found in couple’s Brisbane bathroom leads to creative bid to reunite bird with tattoo artist

Geoff and Delwyn Ballantyne went out for an evening coffee. When they returned they found Hex admiring himself in the mirror

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A peacock that seemingly fell hopelessly in love with himself in a bathroom mirror after breaking into a Brisbane house remains on the run after being evicted.

Geoff and Delwyn Ballantyne left their Alexandra Hills home last week for an evening coffee and returned to find a fully grown peacock gazing adoringly at himself in their bathroom mirror.

“[We] assume the bird was in the front yard and as we opened the garage door he must have walked in,” Geoff said. “Because when we got in, there he was, admiring himself.”

Delwyn was the first to spot the bird and responded with alarm.

“There was a lot of screaming from my wife. She thought there must have been an intruder but then she says it’s a peacock,” Geoff said. “I did believe her, but I tried not to, because it wasn’t very believable.”

The couple attempted to break the visitor’s steadfast gazing into the mirror by offering it water and crackers. When that failed, wildlife rescuers were called in.

They wrangled the peacock into a towel then released it into nearby bushland. Since then the bird has been paying afternoon visits to Suzie Tafolo’s acreage home – also in Alexandra Hills.

Tafolo suspected the bird might have belonged to a local tattoo artist, Elliot Ross, who was looking for his missing peacock, Hex. Ross subsequently confirmed the bird in the Ballantynes’ bathroom and at Tafolo’s property was Hex, due to his unusual plumage.

“I’ve Googled how to catch a peacock, which is something I never thought I’d have to do,” Tafolo said on Monday. “One of the things it says is that they love mirrors, which makes sense in terms of how he ended up in the bathroom.”

Tafolo carted a full-length mirror from her house to the back shed where Hex has been turning up each day.

Hex appeared to take an interest in his reflection on Tuesday and Tafolo said she was hopeful he could soon be reunited with Ross who has owned the bird for a couple of years.

“This is the first time he’s gone walkabout besides going into our neighbour’s property,” Ross said.

“We’ve got a female here as well and she’s stayed. I’m guessing he’s gone walkabout trying to find another female. We’re hoping to try to catch him.”

Griffith University professor emeritus Darryl Jones, an urban ecologist and author of Wild Neighbours, has studied how birds respond to their reflections.

He believes Hex might have been lonely – but not necessarily looking for love.

“Most birds would see it as a rival or a threat or something like that, and usually fight it. But this bird might be just lonely,” he said.

“It’s pretty unusual for two males to be hanging out together, but they will sometimes. Peacocks are not that smart. He might be dumb – but he knows what a female looks like.”

#108. Shops looted as German cost-of-living protests turn violent

Watch: Protesters clash with police and shops looted in Germany

Rioters looted shops in central Frankfurt late on Saturday as clashes erupted in German cities over the cost of living.

Besides Frankfurt - Germany's financial hub - there were incidents of violence in Berlin, Cologne and Hamburg, with police saying they used water cannon to disperse protesters who were targeting emergency workers with fireworks, stones and bottles.

Thousands joined largely peaceful demonstrations earlier in the day. They were organised by the campaign group Gen Z regelt - meaning "Gen Z takes charge" - to protest against underfunding in schools, the high cost of living, public spending cuts and military service.

The unrest in Germany follows similar youth-led mass protests in France, where more than 320,000 people also demonstrated on Saturday.

On Sunday, Germany's Interior Minister Alexander Dobrindt told Bild newspaper that overnight police had prevented "violence like that seen in Paris" in recent weeks and called for "harsh punishments" for rioters and looters.

Overnight, a large section of Frankfurt's main shopping street was closed off by police. A police spokesperson said many arrests were made in the city.

Authorities in Frankfurt, Munich and Berlin reported as many as 18,000 protesters, roughly half of what the organisers had estimated on their website. German media say tens of thousands protested in German cities on Saturday.

Gen Z regelt spokesperson Lina Friedrich said the demonstrations were "to fight for peace and freedom, rather than for war and coercion".

She said: "What matters most to us is better support for the health and education systems. We are against compulsory military service and we are in favour of a fairer distribution of the burden."

Police detain a demonstrator dressed as Batman in Frankfurt

Europe's youth is on the march - but does anything actually unite them?

On Saturday night Frankfurt police said "violent riots" were occurring in the city centre and that special police units had been deployed.

The windows of several shops had been smashed and molotov cocktails were thrown at emergency services, police said.

Officials said the windows of a gun shop were smashed and that they had seized two weapons after arresting a suspect. People also broke into other shops, stealing items like trainers.

Roman Poseck, interior minister of Hesse state, of which Frankfurt is the biggest city, said that "around 100 clearly identified left-wing extremists" had infiltrated the protest in the city. He added that it was "shameful" how the protests had ended.

"We all have those images from France in our minds - looting, burning houses, burning police vehicles, and attacks on police officers... France must not be held up as a model in this regard."

Police said the situation calmed down overnight into Sunday in the city.

People try to break through a window of a Frankfurt shop selling weapons

Similar instances took place in other German cities, following largely peaceful demonstrations.

In Cologne, police said officers were attacked near the main train station, with bottles thrown and a bicycle launched.

Cologne's police chief Johannes Hermanns said it was "deeply saddening that young people who wanted to peacefully demonstrate for their future were infiltrated today by extremists who had nothing but violence in mind".

A smaller demonstration in the evening in Berlin - following larger protests in the day - resulted in 27 police officers being injured, and up to 56 people temporarily detained, Berlin police said.

In Hamburg, people dressed in black set off fireworks and set barricades on fire, with public broadcaster NDR reporting that 120 people were arrested.

The protests in Germany took place on the same day as other large-scale demonstrations in Europe.

In France, more than 320,000 people took part in a third consecutive weekend of largely peaceful protests in cities across the country, demanding action on the high cost of living and poor public services, especially in the education sector.

Thousands of police officers were deployed across the country, and officers fired tear gas at demonstrators in some cities.

There were more than 770 arrests across France during the demonstrations on Saturday, roughly half as many as the previous week's protests, the French interior ministry said.

Thousands demonstrate in Paris as protests spread across Europe

French Interior Minister Laurent NuÃ±ez said a police officer had been injured by a molotov cocktail and French prosecutors later launched an attempted murder probe.

Police in Paris said several hundred rioters had attacked security forces with projectiles.

French protest organisers, the Gilet Jaune, or Yellow Vests, have said dozens of protesters have been seriously injured during recent demonstrations across the country, with many hurt by "grenades".

France suspended the use of one type of stun grenade while policing student protests in early October after a 15-year-old's hand was blown off, but French newspaper Le Monde has reported, external that police have continued to use a different model in subsequent protests.

Protests also took place in Italy, with some clashes between police and demonstrators in Rome and Turin.

In Portugal, police moved to clear a new tent camp that had been assembled after thousands marched to protest against a rental bill that they argue could worsen a housing crisis in the country, where house prices have more than doubled over the past decade.

Mostly young protesters have recently taken to the streets in a swathe of European nations: France, Belgium, Ireland, Spain, Portugal, Italy, Greece, Serbia and Albania.

While the specific demands vary from country to country, the protesters have largely focused on cost-of-living issues, such as access to housing, the quality of education and cuts to public services.

#105. Judge pauses Alan Jones indecent assault trial so former broadcaster can watch Melbourne Cup

Jones, a former radio broadcaster, has pleaded not guilty to 21 charges of indecent assault and sexual touching

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The evidence of a star reporter whose investigation led to charges against Alan Jones was paused so the former 2GB broadcaster and horse racing enthusiast could watch the Melbourne Cup.

Jones’s defence barrister, Gabrielle Bashir SC, asked judge Glenn Walsh if the court could break just before 3pm to watch the race as Jones sat laughing loudly in the front row of the public gallery.

Walsh smiled as he said he knew there was “a particular race” the defendant would be interested in and agreed to break from 2.50pm until 3.10pm. The court commonly will adjourn mid-afternoon to give witnesses a short break.

The Sydney Morning Herald’s chief investigative reporter, Kate McClymont, was in the witness box all day answering questions about her years-long investigation of Jones which was published in 2023.

Under cross-examination by Bashir, McClymont agreed that she used the expression “go the grope” in her interview with complainant C, but said the alleged victim had repeated the phrase.

“You’re not looking for sexy phrases to put in a story, you’re having a conversation with someone,” she said.

McClymont told the court that readership and advertising metrics were of no relevance to her as a journalist. She had been asked if “special investigations under your name sell newspapers”.

Bashir took the witness to call logs and emails between her and her colleagues, including columnist Peter FitzSimons and reporter Jennifer Duke dating back to 2017.

After speaking to a man who made allegations about Jones, known as complainant C in the trial, McClymont wrote: “We have another one.”

In her opening statement Bashir said McClymont and FitzSimons were “looking for a story” and their reporting was fuelled by the hatred and jealousy of his 2GB radio rival Ray Hadley.

McClymont said FitzSimons had been working on a story about allegations against Jones since October 2017 and it was a “long-running matter”.

Earlier, McClymont said the three alleged victims of Jones that she interviewed received no benefit from her or the media company for telling their stories.

McClymont, whose articles led to other alleged victims coming forward, is giving evidence in the lengthy trial of the former broadcaster, who is facing 21 charges of indecent assault and sexual touching against five men.

Several men detailed claims of indecent assault against Jones in McClymont’s articles, including that he groped or inappropriately touched them without their consent.

Police say they formed Strike Force Bonnefin after McClymont’s reporting in the Herald and the Age resulted in alleged victims coming forward.

At the Downing Centre local court on Tuesday, Jones, 85, was supported by men’s rights activist Bettina Arndt and defamation lawyer Paul Svilans from Mark O’Brien Legal.

McClymont, who has won 10 Walkley awards for excellence in journalism, was supported by the editor of the SMH, Jordan Baker, and the deputy editor, Nick Ralston.

McClymont told the court she always sought comment from Jones and his legal team before publication via text and email. In her evidence she agreed with prosecutor Georgina Wright that she spoke to three alleged victims but kept their stories confidential from each other.

McClymont did not initially share her interview notes or recordings with police because they were confidential or not available but received a subpoena for the material in 2026, the court heard.

The strike force was formed after the December 2023 publication of McClymont’s years-long investigation of Jones.

His long radio career came to an abrupt end in 2020 after a series of on-air incidents led to the radio station losing advertisers and haemorrhaging revenue.

When Jones was arrested by New South Wales police after a “long, thorough, protracted” investigation at his city apartment in late 2024, he had been retired from his $4m-a-year breakfast radio show on 2GB for four years.

#103. University of Cambridge scientist among those who died in Spanish train crash

Dr Marta Shahbazi, pictured in 2024, was travelling to collect a prize at an awards ceremony in her native Spain when she died

An award-winning University of Cambridge researcher died in the Spanish train disaster while on her way to collect a prize for scientific excellence.

Dr Marta Shahbazi was travelling to an awards ceremony on Wednesday when the train she was on crashed into another near Urda, south of Madrid, killing 69 people.

The mother of two was an affiliate associate professor leading a group of researchers at MRC Laboratory of Molecular Biology (LMB) in Cambridge and had earned several awards for work on human embryos and cell biology.

In a statement, the LMB paid tribute to the "inspiring, talented and passionate scientist" and described her death as a "profound shock and a huge loss".

The expert in cell biology ran the Shahbazi Laboratory at the LMB, based on the Cambridge Biomedical Campus.

She had been announced as the winner of a Spanish Society for Developmental Biology award for her contributions to the study of early human development and was due to collect the award at a conference in El Rompido in Andalusia.

The LMB said: "As a wife and mother of two young children, Marta wrote eloquently about balancing her family and career, serving as a much-admired role model for a 'mother scientist', a term she used to describe herself.

"Marta was an inspiring, talented and passionate scientist whose research shed light on the earliest steps in the development of human embryos and how errors occur that lead to failed pregnancies," the LMB said.

The institute said her death was "a profound shock and a huge loss for LMB".

Her work had been recognised with "many awards and prizes", the laboratory added.

Shahbazi joined the Cambridge laboratory in 2020

Shahbazi is listed on the University of Cambridge website as a principal investigator at its Stem Cell Institute (CSCI).

Institute director Prof Bertie Gottgens said its community was "shocked and deeply saddened" by her death.

"Marta led her primary research group at the MRC Laboratory of Molecular Biology (LMB) alongside her roles as an affiliate principal investigator and affiliate assistant professor at the CSCI," he said.

"Across both institutes, she was highly respected for her pioneering research on early human development and valued for her collaborative spirit.

"Her creativity, passion, and generosity will be greatly missed."

The news of her death was first confirmed in a social media post, external by the CiMUS, based in Santiago de Compostela where Shahbazi's aunt works.

Shahbazi undertook her PhD at the Spanish National Cancer Research Centre, before joining the University of Cambridge as a post-doctoral fellow and then the LMB in 2020.

The crash in Spain happened when a state-operated Renfe high-speed train smashed into the back of a stationary train operated by the Iryo company.

It is one of the worst rail disasters in Europe's modern history, and Spain's worst crash since 2013.

Three days of national mourning were declared in the country.

More than 540 people were on board both trains.

#102. Bullock doesn’t see ‘economy cracking at this point’ – as it happened

RBA hikes cash rate for fifth time in blow to mortgage holders

Use our loan calculator to check how the rate rise may affect you

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

What a jam-packed afternoon. Let’s recap the top things that happened:

The Reserve Bank has hiked rates for the fifth time this year, from 4.6% to 4.85%. It means the cash rate has now reached its highest level since 2008.

The decision was not a unanimous one: six members of the RBA board voted to raise the cash rate, while three voted to leave it unchanged.

The treasurer, Jim Chalmers, acknowledged the rate hike will be “difficult news” for Australians already paying a “hefty price”.

The RBA governor, Michele Bullock, said it was “not the news that many Australians want to hear”, but it would prevent “even worse pain down the track”.

The Australian stock market ended higher after the central bank did what it was expected to do and hiked interest rates for the fifth time this calendar year.

For those who are wondering how the announcement could affect you, you can use our loan calculator to find out. And to read more, check out our full report.

Tim Wilson rejects Gallagher’s blaming of parliament for lack of savings policy

The shadow treasurer, Tim Wilson, has rejected Katy Gallagher’s accusation that parliament is to blame for the lack of spending-saving policies.

Speaking to ABC’s Afternoon Briefing, he said:

double quotation markWe wrote to the government at the start of the year and said: let’s have a bipartisan approach to reducing government spending. The government rejected that.

Every time we have asked them to audit government expenditure to see if it is financing organised crime, they have rejected that.

Wilson said he does not believe the government is committed to delivering net savings.

double quotation markThey will never commit to that because, as we know and history shows, for every $2 of savings they claim to find, they go on and spend another $3, and it ends up only stoking inflation further.

Katy Gallagher says parliament to blame for lack of government savings

The finance minister, Katy Gallagher, has blamed the parliament for blocking the government’s attempts to introduce spending-saving policies.

Speaking to ABC’s Afternoon Briefing, the Labor minister said:

double quotation markI hear a lot of calls for cuts to government spending. I don’t hear as many specifics about where people would like to see that come from.

In fact, we’ve got savings in the parliament now that the parliament is refusing to pass.

Asked whether the government intends to deliver net savings in the next budget, Gallagher said:

double quotation markWe’ll be making responsible savings decisions, in line with the job we have to do. I say, in the last two updates, our policy decisions line has contributed positively to the budget overall. That shows you the approach that Jim [Chalmers] and I have taken, and we’ll continue to bring that approach to the decisions we’ve got over the next few weeks as we finalise them.

It’s not unusual for a finance minister, or a treasurer, or a prime minister to have an ambitious appetite around savings, but we need to work through our processes respectfully.

Bullock says she doesn’t “see the economy cracking at this point” even though some people will feel like they are experiencing a recession.

The decision to raise interest rates for a fifth time this year will face inevitable accusations the RBA is pushing the country into an economic downturn.

Bullock pushes back against that framing:

double quotation markOur central forecasts do not see us going into recession, but it does see growth slowing quite markedly.

It shows that consumption is quite sluggish, and I think that is going to make people feel like it’s a recession because they feel poorer, their spending can’t grow very quickly because their incomes aren’t growing very quickly.

The Australian stock market ended higher after the central bank did what it was expected to do and hiked interest rates for the fifth time this calendar year.

The main S&P/ASX200 index rose 30.3 points, or 0.3%, to 8886.8, as the broader All Ordinaries gained 31.7 points, or 0.4%, to 9008.9.

Most of the market’s 11 sectors were in the green, with the major materials and consumer stocks leading the way, while the energy sector carried most of the loss-makers.

The Reserve Bank board ended its two-day meeting on monetary policy on Tuesday with a 25 basis point rise in the cash interest rate to 4.85%.

The Australian dollar was buying 69.77 US cents, down from 70.21 US cents at 5pm on Monday.

Bullock asked about effect of US-Israel war in Iran on interest rates

Guardian Australia’s economics editor, Patrick Commins, asks Bullock whether the RBA would have raised rates if oil prices had stayed low.

In other words, what would have happened had the US and Israel not launched strikes against Iran?

Bullock gives a nuanced response, pointing out that Australia already had a problem with “excess demand” that required cooling.

double quotation markNow, would that have meant that we had to go up the same amount? I don’t know the answer to that.

It’s possible that it’s caused interest rates to mean they have to be a little bit higher.

Bullock goes on to articulate an economic risk posed by raising inflation expectations caused by high oil prices: as consumers resign themselves to higher prices, businesses lift theirs ahead of anticipated rising costs, creating a self-fulfilling loop that entrenches inflation.

RBA independence from government ‘a two-way street’, Bullock says

Michele Bullock has described independence as a “two-way street”, noting that she doesn’t tell the government what to spend on, and that the government doesn’t tell the RBA what to do with rates.

The role of public sector spending in fuelling inflation looms large, with Labor under pressure from critics over five rate hikes this year.

Bullock says that governments need to decide on how much spending is required to provide for the nation. She says:

double quotation markBut that is for governments to decide. Independence is a two-way street. I don’t tell them what to do with fiscal policy. They don’t tell me what to do with interest rates.

Unemployment and housing concerns behind board split: RBA governor

More from Michele Bullock’s press conference:

The RBA decision to raise rates was split, with three members of the rate-setting board preferring a hold, while six voted to raise.

Bullock says those in favour of a hold were concerned about risks to the economy, including rising unemployment and housing weakness. Bullock said:

double quotation markThey felt that there were potential downside risks in these circumstances, particularly since we’d already done four interest rate rises, and maybe we should wait a bit longer to get some more data to test that.

Bullock says market watchers shouldn’t interpret the split decision as a sign the RBA won’t raise further if inflation risks materialise.

The RBA governor, Michele Bullock, says “this is not the news that many Australians want to hear, but it is what’s needed to get inflation down”.

She says “some pain now will prevent even worse pain down the track”.

double quotation markI understand that today’s decision is going to be difficult for Australians with mortgages, many of whom are already managing large repayments.

But households with mortgages are not the only Australians doing it tough. Every household is dealing with higher prices for groceries, electricity, insurance, and other essentials.

High inflation is particularly painful for Australians on lower incomes, including people who rent and have little or no savings to fall back on.

The comments were made in opening remarks to journalists in Sydney ahead of questions.

Several economists are interpreting the RBA’s statement accompanying the rate hike as “dovish”, which means further hikes are less likely.

UBS economists describe the decision as “clearly dovish” while Anthony Malouf, economist at Ebury, says there is an “absence of a clear tightening bias”.

The Australian dollar also immediately dropped against its US counterpart after the decision, which shows traders do not believe the central bank is, at this stage, looking to hike much further.

This could all change if inflation remains elevated, but for the moment, some economists and currency traders believe this could be the last hike, at least for a while.

Chalmers was asked about the federal budget and its impact on inflation. He said:

double quotation markOur budgets are not what’s putting upward pressure on inflation. But we can play a helpful role and that’s what we intend to do.

He was also asked if he’d consider stepping aside.

Jim Chalmers, the treasurer, is speaking now after the RBA’s decision. He said:

double quotation markToday’s decision was widely expected, but that doesn’t make it any easier for Australian families and businesses to absorb. It will be difficult news for many Australians who are already paying a hefty price for circumstances outside of their control.

He pointed to the ongoing war in the Middle East as a major factor in inflation:

double quotation markThe war’s in its ninth month and its re-escalation is putting sustained upward pressure on inflation.

It’s weighing on growth and its end can’t come soon enough for the global economy or for Australians facing cost of living pressures.

Macquarie Bank announces it will pass rate rise on

Macquarie Bank has already announced it will pass on the interest rate rise to mortgage holders.

The bank said it will increase variable home loans by 0.25%, effective 19 November. It will also increase ongoing variable rates paid on its transactions and savings accounts by 0.25%, a boon to savers. Ben Perham, the head of personal banking, said:

double quotation markWith household budgets already feeling the strain, today’s decision by the RBA will be challenging for many Australians.

To help our variable home loan customers prepare, we’re again waiting more than two weeks before this latest increase takes effect.

The cash rate is now the highest it has been in 18 years

The decision was not a unanimous one: six members of the RBA board voted to raise the cash rate, while three voted to leave it unchanged.

double quotation markThe Board is conscious of the period in which inflation has been too high. Given the revised inflation outlook and the continuing upside risks, the Board judged that a further tightening in financial conditions is warranted to support the return of inflation to target.

The Board will continue to do what it considers necessary to bring inflation sustainably back to target.

#100. Armed police start patrols in Sydney after Bondi Beach attack

Armed police officers on patrol outside Sydney's Luna Park

Heavily-armed police officers have started patrolling Sydney's streets, as part of heightened security measures following last year's deadly shooting at Bondi Beach.

The Armed Response Command (ARC) will see specially trained officers in busy tourist areas carrying long-arm rifles â an unfamiliar sight for most residents.

New South Wales police say the officers will also patrol major events like sports matches and places of worship "to prevent, deter and rapidly respond to serious threats, including active armed offender incidents".

Fifteen people - including a 10-year-old girl â were killed in December when two gunmen opened fire on an event marking the Jewish festival of Hanukkah at the popular beach.

The attack was Australia's worst mass shooting in almost three decades and prompted sweeping gun law reforms and a crackdown on hate speech.

One resident told ABC news that the sight of armed police was "unnerving".

"They are just everywhere. Those guns are huge."

But another woman told the broadcaster that seeing police with weapons made her feel safe.

ARC is specifically designed to be "highly visible across Sydney, driving specialist vehicles and carrying rifles," New South Wales police said in a statement.

Its role "is built on a simple principle - preserving life," Detective Superintendent Andrew Evans said.

The shooting on 14 December put huge pressure on Prime Minister Anthony Albanese's government, with calls for immediate action on antisemitism.

Jewish communities have welcomed the new police operation, which will eventually consist of 250 officers, but others have called for safeguards to protect human rights.

"No Australian wants to see officers with military weapons on our streets, but the sight of body bags is far worse," Alex Ryvchin, co-CEO of Executive Council of Australian Jewry, said.

But Amnesty International Australia said it was concerned about the "militarisation of policing", and asked for assurances from the state government that ARC will not be used to suppress peaceful protests.

"Any expansion of police powers and capabilities must be accompanied by clear, publicly accessible rules, robust independent oversight and accountability," said the organisation's Andrew Witheford.

The New South Wales administration defended the new measures.

"Our world changed forever. That's just the truth of it, right across this country. And I said it at the time, we will never be the same again," the state's Police Minister Yasmin Catley told the ABC.

One of the alleged gunmen, Naveed Akram, has been charged with 78 offences including 15 counts of murder, 40 counts of attempted murder, and one count of committing a terrorist act.

His father, Sajid Akram, 50 - who was also armed and shot at the crowd on Bondi Beach - was killed by police at the scene.

A national tragedy 30 years ago united Australia. Why hasn't the Bondi shooting?
